PT,F,ASANT PARAGRAPHS.

PBEVIOUS.—Lawyer : "You will get your third out of the estate, madam." Widow: Oh, Mr. Bluebags ! How can you say such a thing, with my second hardly cold in his grave ? " AN ANCIENT HOCSE.—Dashaway : "I had no idea that your family was such an old one." Travers (proudly) : "I should say it was. Why, old man, we have some bills dating back four generations ! " A CULINARY GRADUATE.—Grandma "And your mamma cooks nice things for dinner herself, does she? Aud does papa say grace when he sits down at table?" Tommy: "Well, he says Gracious ! ' " _ _ _

THE GUEST CHAMBER. -" So this beautifully- furnished room is your guest chamber ? " remarked Mr. Heights, admiringly. "Yes," responded Mr. Isolite, of Lonesomehurst ; "that is where our servant-girl sleeps, when we have one." Mistress: "Bridget, how many policemen did you have in the kitchen last night ? " Bridget (modestly) : " Only foive, mum." Mistress: "Couldn't you induce one of them to stay all night? You know I am afraid of burglars." Bridget (brightening) : " Rist aisy, mum; three av 'em shlapes here regular."

THE FAMILY MAN.

He drives the nail in silence grim, But he is far from dumb. You know it by the sound from him Each time he hits his thumb. LIKELY TO REMEMIIER.—Proud Mother "You beffen't ki.Fsed the baby." Bachelor Uncle: "Um try to reniembe. next time. "I'll kiss her when I—er—come back from Europe." "When will that be ?" "Let—me—see. About sixteen years." A DRAMATIC TRIUMPH AT LAST. Lobby- lounger : " How was the play last night ? " Firstriter : "Wonderful ! Most artistic and dra- matic production seen for years. Held the audience spellbound from first to last. Why, sir, in some of the thrilling situations there were times when not a sound could be heard but the hard breathing of Othello, the suppressed sobs of Desde- inona, and the conversation in the boxes." "I AM very much better this morning," said MTS. M'Bride. "I feel like a new woman." "I hope you won't dress like one," replied her has- band, fervently. A GENTLE SARCASM.—A Connecticut man onoe called at a farmhouse, and was asked by the farmer to take a glass of eider which he had made from his own apples. After drinking the cider, the man didn't exactly smack his lips, but turning to the farmer, said: "Pity you hadn't another apple." "Why?" asked the farmer. "Oh, you could have made another barrel of it!" was the answer. ON THE WRONG TACK.—The villain gnashed his gleaming teeth with a grating sound. Ha, ha! my proud beauty," he hissed, "I will yet bring you to the dust !" The proud beauty smiled a wan little smile. " Never, ' she twittered. " You are on the wrong tack. The man to win me must bang the dust to me." EASILY ANSWERED. —" Why," shouted the impass;oned orator, "why is it that the wives of ore farmers lack the happy, contented look to be found on the face of the bareheaded peasant- woman of the Continent ? " "Because," said the man near the door, "because the bareheaded peasant-woman does not have to worry all the time ; about her hat being on straight." NOT HER SPHERE.—Mrs. Cobwigger : "I don't see why I shouldn't be considered in regard to the new house we move into." Cobwigger : "Why, my dear, I don't see what difference it can make to you. Since you have joined all the women's clubs you are never at home." EVIDENCE.—Miss Summit: "Mr. Ja,gway called on me last night, and I think he had been drink- ing." Miss Pallisade : "He told me this morning that you looked beautiful." HOME TRAINING.—" Are you married" said the manager to a man who was looking for a situation. "No, sir." "Then I can't employ you. I have a place into which I could put you, but we engage only married men." "May I ask the reason of this discrimination? Is it that families may be provided with support ! " "Not at all. It is because we find that married men know better how to obey than bachelors." Miss Getthere (enthusiastic worker at chutch bazaar) : "Now, Mr. Slimpurse, you really must take a chance in this beautiful pipe; you really must. Just think, the pipe is worth twenty shil- lings, and the chances are only one shilling each. Mr. Slimpurse (edging off) : "Very sorry, miss, but I don't smoke." Miss Getthere: "Oh, but you can learn, you know." Mr. Slimpurse : "Tobacco does not agree with me. I would have no earthly use for a pipe." Miss Getthere (struck with a bright idea) : "Well, there isn't the slightest probability of your drawing it, you know." "THERE was quite a fight in front of our place to-day," said a business man at the supper table. "Two men got into a row, one struck the other, and then the crowd gathered. The man who was struck ran and grrtbbed an ugly-looking stick, and rushed back, his eyes blazing. I thought sure he'd knock the other man's brains out, and I stepped right in between them." The young heir had given over eating his tart as the narrative pro- ceeded, and his eyes leaned right out of his head. He was proud of his father's valour and he cried— "He couldn't knock any brains out of you, could he, father ? " The father looked long and curiously at his son and heir, but the lad's countenance was frank and innocent and open. When it closed with the tart on the inside the father gasped slightly and resumed his supper.

Too GOOD A FELLOW.—Years ago there was a very queer old clergyman in the diocese of Canter- bury, of whom a hundred anecdotes are told. On his going one (lay to order a wig, the barber, young in business, readily accepted the order, and then ventured to ask his guest to dine. The invitation was accepted, and a bowl of punch followed, to which the divine did full justice. After it was over, the barber was beginning to measure, but his customer shook his head. "No, no," he said, "you sha'n't make the wig." "Why not?" exclaimed the astonished host. "Have I done anything to offend you?" "Quite the contrary; you're such a good fellow that I haven't got the heart to take you in. I'll go to somebody else. You know, if you'd made the wig, you'd never have been paid for it !" WANTED A GRAPHIC ACCOUNT.—A short time ago the assistant at a railway bookstall in a large city was surprised by a gentleman who rushed on to the platform and breathlessly asked for the paper with the most graphic account of the China and Japan war. The assistant, noticing the excited and nervous condition of his customer, immediately complied with his request to the best of his ability. The stranger, sharply turning over the leaves of his purchase, gasped— "Ah ! I've got it at last." The man of specials," wishing to know the cause of his trouble, asked— "Have you any friendss or relations engaged in the war?" "Oh, no ! " said the stranger. " I'm a grocer; I'm in search of a name for our new blend of tea at one-and-aiY-"

" CAPITAL " CHAT.

I have reason to believe that before the Arch- bishop of Canterbury requested the Prime Minister to appoint a Parliamentary Committee to inquire into the question of Licensing Reform, be satisfied himself that the request would be complied with. In that case the interest of the public will centre in the constitution of the committee. It is antici- pated that representative men of all shades of opinion will be invited to join it, though how in the world Sir Wilfrid Lawson, Mr. COSMO Bonsor, Mr. J. S. Talbot, and Mr. M'Ewan are going to agree upon a report one cannot imagine. A great deal will depend upon the choice of chairman. The Government, however, will be duly grateful to the Primate for getting rid of the question for at least the whole of the session.

It is not often the Local Government Board has to deal with cases of cholera in England,but it had this experience on Saturday, when official report was made that a steamship, the Garlands, had put into Holyhead harbour with several cases of cholera on board. Two ot the seamen had died on the way from the Black Sea and Constantinople. Dr. Thorne instantly sent Dr. Theodore Thomson, one of his medical inspectors, to Holyhead, and after proper care in disinfectants.the Garlands was allowed to proceed to Barrow-in-Furness,where she will be closely watched by the local medical offi- cers and the people of that port. She had 14 cases of cholera coming from the Black Sea.

The announcement that the Government did not intend to deal with the question of secondary education this session receives fresh importance from the circumstance that the Education Depart- ment has now completed its plan for the delimita- tion of the spheres of elementary and secondary instruction. This plan has been forwarded to the Cabinet for consideration; but so very thorny a subject will not be taken up so long as foreign pro- blems continue to crowd upon us. It may be stated, however, that the plan will have a very deep-reaching and serious effect upon all the higher grade board schools throughout the country. The board and voluntary schools will be practically re- duced to purely elementary functions, so as to prevent the elementary from overlapping the se- condary system. But the details have not leaked out. There is every ground for thinking,however, that board schools as now understood in large centres of population, where they are doing a large portion of the work of a secondary system, have a period of strain and trouble before them.

Mr. T. H. Bolton, who made so very striking a political somersault from Liberalism into Liberal Unionism during the last Parliament, is not being allowed to enter upon the reward of his conver- sion to Unienism without criticism,any more than Mr. Austin has been allowed to receive the wreath of the Poet Laureate in peace. As the latter ap- pointment was regarded by the Radicals,and a great many Tories as well, as a pure party appointment, so Mr. Bolton's appointment as Taxing Master is denounced in Radical circles as a political job. If Mr. Bolton bad gone over to Unionism at the time of the great split along with so many other solici- tors, it is quite probable that he would not have re- ceived the office now conferred upon him ; but neither would there have been the bitter abuse and contumely which have been showered upon him over since be began to waver in his allegiance to Mr. Gladstone. It must be said for him that his change of front was a matter of conscientious con- vi3tion, and without any hope of a future reward. He is a solicitor in large practice, a man of strict integrity and high honour: and it is not customary for any political party to give its loaves and fishes to its opponents.

Mr. Chamberlain's Tory evolution is proceeding apace. On the Friday before the opening of Par- liament he is to be the guest of the Constitutional Club, the Conservative rival of the National Liberal. It has even been suggested that he should be made an honorary member of the club, and stranger things have happened. This will not be the first function connected with Conservative clubs in which he has taken part in different districts ot the country, but he has not hitherto been associated with any demonstration of the kind in London. The hospitality extended to him in this instance, and its acceptance by him, will help to bridge the gulf be- tween the two wings of the Ministerialists, and what is ostentatiously done in the very centre of the metropolis by the leading lights of Unionism may be imitated in the provinces by the smaller luminaries. The precedent at least is expected to be followed by local politicians. such a compli- mentary dinner has long been spoken of. It was partly arranged for even before the defeat of the late Government, but that event gave a new turn both to the thoughts and interests of Tories and Liberal Unionists alike, and the proposal fell through until lately, when it has been again revived.

The electoral situation at Southampton is very curious and complicated. Mr. Candy, Q.C., was selected to fight thili Tory battle, and he is still only a provisional candidate, and prepared to make way for Mr. Tankerville Chamberlayne should that gentleman be declared eligible. It is satisfactory to learn that there is a probability of the Liberal and Labour parties arriving at an agreement. The basis of the suggested compromise is that the Labour party refrain from opposing Sir F. Evans should he be selected as the Liberal candidate, and, on the other hand,that no second Liberal should be run at the next general election against the Labour nominee. If this arrangement is carried out, the local Liberals profess themselves confident of carry- ing the seat, as very mans of the 800 odd who sup- ported Mr. J. R. Macdonald, the Independent can- didate, in July last, are disaffected Radicals, who will give no countenance to Mr. Gibson, who is standing in the interest of the Social Democratic Federation.

The allegations sent from Bloemfontein that the Orange Free State delegates to Pretoria had access to documents definitely revealing the fact that Jameson's raid was part of a carefully organised conspiracy against Dutch rule in South Africa are highly sensational, and are received with credulous belief by the many who are ready to believe every evil of the Chartered Company. But notwithstand- ing the apparent definiteness of the statements, it must be remarked that there is opportunity of sub- mitting the documents in questbn to the examina- tions of impartial experts. the public is bound to keep an open mind, free from temptations to pre- judgment, remembering that nothing whatever is at present known as to the value of the alleged evi- dence.• It is a safe rule to receive with many reservations everything bearing the marks of Boer inspiration, and that is especially the case in this instance, since the plot, as described, altogether conflicts with the policy Mr. Cecil Rhodes has long been pursuing.

The more one learns about Lord Salisbury's new Convention with France in regard to Siam,the more surprising and inexplicable it seems. I have men- tioned more than once the corresponding question with China as to the conduct of the latter in ceding to France two districts of Kiang Hung which she I was under an engagement to Great Britain to cede to no foreign Power, I have also stated in detail the demands which Lord Salisbury made on Chita in consequence of this breach of faith. Now it might be thought that, as we had given up the east side of the Mekong to France and a very large part of Siam to boot, it mattered little to us now that China had given up two important districts, and that we might well drop this claim on beaten and helpless China when we had dropped so much in favour of France that we need not have done. But lam in- formed that this is not the case, and that our claim on China is to be pressed forward energetically,and, if need be, enforced by armed occupation of the ter- ritories we demand. If this be the case, it would seem that our diplomacy is wonderfully strong and inflexible in the East only when we are dealing with the helpless, and very much the o'her way when the powerful are opposed to US. Nothing on the east of the Mekong now matters to us, and yet we are persisting in enforcing a very severe and humiliating punishment on the Chinese for some- thing which they did there—ft thing, indeed, which was quite indefensible, but which after our collapse iu Siam has done us no injury and does not affect 118 in the least.

One other demand which Lord Salisbury has made on China is less open to crit,cism. This is that the West River, which rises in Yunnan and enters the sea at Canton, after flowing almost parallel to the Tonquin border, shall be opened to foreign trade and navigation, with two ports on it. This is a very important matter for British trade in Southern China, as very many of our Chambers of Commerce have long perceived. I am glad to be able to say that no difficulty is anticipated in the way of obtaining this commercial boon.

THE 1-AAMINER, SATURDAY JANUARY 25. 1896

Brocades of silk, enriched by gold and silver were made in China 8.C., 1721. It is stated that nearly 1,000,000 pounds of fur for hatters' purposes are produced in the United States. It is said that one of Lord Salisbury's daughters may be seen cycling, in all weathers, in the vicinity of Hat- field. _ _ _

The wood of the lime tree is used for the sounding boards of pianofortes, as it does not warp under change of temperature or atmosphere. Queen Margherita of Italy is about to make her first appearance as an author, by the publication of her experiences as an Alpine climber. Over two million bottles' of the value of seven thou- sand pounds, are recovered each year from the dust yards in London' and returned to their owners. It is estimated that in the metropolitan area some four hundred and eighty tons of sulphur are emitted daily into the atmosphere from seven hundred thousand dwelling-houses. No wonder the fogs are what they are. Since the death of her son, the Prince Impelial„ the ex-Empress Euganie has never worn coloured dresses. When at home she prefers black cashmere to all other dress fabrics, but when travelling she wears the hand- somest of black velvets, satins and silks. Among the Germans the badge of married women con- sists of a little cap or hood, of which they are very proud, and" donning the cap " is a feature of the wed- ding day among the peasants of certain localities. The married women of Little Russia ae always seen, even in the hottest weather, with a thick cloth of dark hue twisted about their head. The unoccupied class at the last Census numbered as fellows :—Retired from business (not Army. Navy, Church, or Medicine),l92,94l males and 68,496 females; pensioners, 20.988 males and 2,799 females; living on own means, 97,466 males and 409,097 females. Others over ten years of age, 1,397,318 males and 6,965,268 females of whom 5,217,700 were over twenty years of age. According to a correspondent of the "Times," the GErman Emperor, in habits, tastes, and sympathies. is more like an Englishman than like a German. Another blow to our national self-esteem. One reason put forth for the exasperating conduct of the Kaiser in the Trans- vaal crisis is very quaint. It is that the Queen of Holland has to be married when she is sixteen,in August this year. Her Majesty is a most eligible match, and it is well known that the German Emperor would gladly secure her band for a member of his house. But the Dutch hate the idea ef a German Prince Consort for their Queen. They have looked towards Denmark; but the engagement of Prince Charles to the Princess Maud of Wales has put out of the question the most desirable engagement that the Queen could have entered into. Now, while the Dutch hate the Germans, they love the Boers, who are of their own blood. Hence it is thought that the German Emperor may have sent his message of congratulation to the Boes ani the insult to England in the hope of currying favour with the Dutch people,so as to pave the way for an alliance with a Prussian Prince, probably a son of Prince Albert of Prussia, who is Prince- Regent of Brunswick.

To Clean a Sewing Machine. If your sewing machine needs cleaning, oil all the bearings with kerosene, used freely. Run your machine fast for a few minutes unthreaded, then wipe off clean; oil with machine oil, and you will be surprised to see how easily it will run and how clean it will look.

Ahead of all Women. Danish women are ahead of any other women on the face of the globe in one particular. They have just an- nounced the opening of a woman's theatre for theLcom- ing winter. The plays are to be written by women dramatists ; every character—yes ! the male characters to be acted by women : orchestra, soloists, chorus, con- ductor, exclusively feminine. Margaret Thorson, the authoress, has completed a play for the theatre, and Frau Enarna Gad prepared a ballet. All this sounds well, so far. So far, but no further perhaps.

Buttered Eggs. Boil six eggs for one half -hour, thea let them stand in cold water for a few minutes. Take shells off and cut each in half. Take out the yolks. Be careful not to break the white. Cut a tiny piece off the round of each half so thatlit will stand up like a cup. Mix the yolk to a smooth paste, with a teaspoonful of dry mustard, one half teaspoonful of salt and one and a half tablespoonful of vinegar. Fill the twelve white pieces with the mix- ture. _

A Doctor Queen. Queen Amelia of Portugal took up the study of medi- cine on account of the ill-health of her royal consort. The king is suffering from a formation of fat around the heart, and, as the complaint increased, lthought the King took plenty of exercise, and as the doctors did not seem to agree upon the treatment, the Queen made up her mind to study the case herself. She has now passed a regular sort of examination before a medical board, and thus duly qualified. She has taken over the treat- ment of the King, who has the fullest confidence in her ability, and who is already deriving benefit by following her orders.

Everybody's Girl—A Man's Description. Said a man lately discoursing to me on girls: "There Is a type of girl that everybody likes. Nobody can tell exactly whys but after you have met her you turn away to some other woman and you say, Don't you like Miss So-and-So?' The reason you like her is a subtle one; without knowing all about her, you feel just the sort of girl she is. She is the girl who is not too bright and good' to be able to find joy and pleasure all over the world. She is the girl who appreciates the fact that she cannot always have the first choice of everything in the world. She is the girl who is not aggressive, and does not find delight in inciting aggressive people. She is the girl who has tact enough not to say the very thing that will cause the skeleton in her friend's closet to rattle his hones. She is the girl who, whether it is warm or cold, clear or stormy, finds mo fault with the weather. When you invite her anywhere she compliments you by looking her best. And when I come to think of it, I might sum up by saying she is the girl who makes you feel she likes you, and therefore you like her.

Some Terrible Facts and figures on the death rate of children are given in "London." To experts and officials the truth is known, but to the mind of the general public the truth of the fearful waste of child life, the wholesale destruction of the future generation, has never come home. In London alone the yearly total of children who die under one year of age is 22,000. Most of these deaths occur in poor dis- tricts, where the children are—probably have to be— neglected by their parents, or are insufficiently or impro- perly fed. The highest infant mortality is in the Strand. Hol born ,St. George's-i n-the-East(Sou th w ark) ,Lirn ehouse, and Clerkenwell, where one out of every five children born dies before it is twelve months old. In Bromley, i out of every 1,000 deaths, 461 are those of children under one year. In Poplar the number of such deaths is 465. In St. George-the-Martyr nearly half the deaths are of children under five years of age. In Limehouse the total deaths last year were 1.396, and of these 616 were children under five years of age. In St. George's- in-the-E tat the total deaths were 951, and, of these, children numbered 472. Diseases produced by living in crowded and insanitary surroundings, want of cleanli- ness, improper food (especially the eating of unripe fruit, tainted meat, and stale vegetables), are the chief causes of this.

LADIES LETTER.

MT DEAREST NELLIE,

I dare not prophesy whether we are going to have skating. I half fear we are not, but at any rate there is no harm in being prepared, is there There is no sport more graceful for a girl, as a man remarked to me the other day, than skating, but however well a girl may skate she will not look graceful if she has not a stylish jacket and gown. So take my hint and go in for some such costume as this: Gown of tan colour trimmed with white fox, with a touch of dark velvet at the waist, and the vest trimmed with lines of white braid; toque of velvet, with an osprey set at one side, and the buttons at the hem of the skirt revealing an under. petticoat of white cloth. Then, for a coat, you might very well have one of those velvet ones. There is nothing handsomer, I think, than black velour du Nord. You know the shape of the sleeves if you have read my previous letters care- fully, but I may tell you the collar should be of fine chinchilla fur. It might have a turn-down velvet collar, you know, and big sterling silver buttons, but I don't think this looks so well as the chinchilla.

A deep turn-down collar of ermine, again, adds much to the appearance of a petunia or gendarme blue cloth cape, and the same snowy fur may also be used to trim fancy velvet muffs, or, if one wishes to be quite in the van of fashion, to line a large skunk, seal or sable one. Oddments of fur of all kinds can be made use of this season, and those who have pieces of any kind lying by them might, by the aid of judicious investments at the sales now proceeding, concoct various modish gar- ments at a very small cost. A smart cape for early spring wear could be of brown ribbed serge made in two tiers, the lower one bordered with a very narrow band of brown fur—musquash, mink, seal or sable—of which there should be revers carried down in narrow points to the edge of the cape in front. Add a high collar of serge turned over at the top with fur, and line the cape first with flannel, then with tailor canvas, and finally with cheap silk or brocade, and you will have a pretty wrap to wear well into the spring. There is as much choice as ever in regard to evening dresses, and I have seen some nice ones since I wrote you last. One, which I know would have pleased you, was made of white satin, with the skirt trimmed on the hem with an accordion kilted flounce of white chiffon headed with a ruche of roses; the bodice of this was cut right off the shoulders, with the sleeves made of short puffs headed with roses, the décolletage being bordered with ruches of tulle. Another very pretty dress was made of black and white striped silk lined with pale pink silk, an effect which was sufficiently obvious when its wearer danced, and this boasted a bodice made of white chiffon covered with black net with an appliqué of white lace upon it traced with silver spangles. The sleeves were formed of frills of black-and-white striped satin ribbon and and box-pleated white chiffon, but these were not permitted to reach to the elbow. Very simply again, is a bodice made of silk crêpe gauze plainly gathered and set into a niche across the front, the shoulder straps being formed of folds of the crêpe caught here and there to form little frills, and terminating with pendant bows and ends of pink satin ribbon.

Here is a nice design for an out-door ctress Have it in purple cloth, with waistcoat and hems of caracule, the buttons of cut steel. If your waist is slim you might venture on a narrow black satin band right round the waist fastened in front with a steel button, which would keep the coat in its place better. For the blouse have one of the new printed velveteens; a pine pattern on a pale ground would be nice. The lace should be cream colour, and the buttons might be Parisian dia- monds.

Toques look better than anything for winter wear by young women. A dainty one, in the style of Henri Quatre, as in black velvet trimmed with jet and feathers, and coifs the head without conceal. ing the hair.

Your loving JENNIE.

The Princess of Wales has nsacie a somewhat curious choice from among the many types of tricycles which have lately been submitted for her inspection. A pattern of machine once popular in the old solid tyre days, but long since entirely discarded, was the one which took the Princess's fancy., chiefly because it is so convenient for mounting. 01 course, the machine had to be speci- ally built, a work probably of no Finall difficulty, as the pattern is obsolete. It is a reculiar loo!,ing machine, having a large driving wheel on one side, and on the other two little steering wheels, one at each end of a long bar, which gives it a rather crab-like appearance. The front is quite open for mounting and dismounting, and the steering wheels are moved by a spade handle at the side.

THE HIDDEN HAND;

CAPITOLA THE MADCAP, BY MRS. E. D. E. N. SOUTHWORTH.

CHAPTER XXI/.

THE SMUGGLER AND CAPITOLA. " Come buy of me! come buy come buy Buy, lads, or else the lasses cry; I have lawns as white as snow; Silk as black as e'er was crow; Gloves as sweet as damask roses, Veils for faces; musk for noses ;- Pins and needles made of steel ; All you need from head to heel." —SHAKESPEARE. "If I am not allowed to walk or ride out alone I shall 'gang.' I know I shall. Was ever such a dull, lonesome, hum-drum place as this same Hurricane Hall ?" complained Cap, as she sat sewing with Mrs. Condiment in the housekeeper's room.

"You don't like this quiet countly life 2" inquired Mrs. Condiment.

"No; no better than I do a quiet country grave- yard. I don't want to return to dust before my time, I tell you," said Cap, yawning dismally over her work.

"I HEAR YOU, VIXEN!" roared the voice of Old Hurricane, who presently came storming in and saying; "If you want a ride go and get ready quickly and come with me; I am going down to the water- mill, please the Lord, to warn Hopkins off the premises, worthless villain 1 had my grain there since yesterday morning, and hasn't sent it home yet I shan't stay in my mill another month. Come, Cap, be off with you and get ready I" The girl did not need a second bidding, but flew to prepare herself, wt.ile the old man ordered the horses. In ten minutes more Capitola and Major War. field cantered away. They had been gone about two hours, and it was almost time to expect their return, and Mrs. Condi- ment had just given orders for the tea-table to be set, when Wool came into her room and said there was a sailor at the hall-door with some beautiful foreign goods which he wished to show to the ladies of the house.

"A sailor. Wool, a sailor with foreign goods for sale? I am very much afraid he's one of these smugglers I've heard tell of; and I'm not sure about the right of buying from smugglers! However, I suppose there's no harm in looking at his goods. You may call him in, Wool," said the old lady, tam- pering with temptation. "He do look like a smudgeler, dat's a fact," said Wool, whose ideas of the said craft were purely

imaginary. "I don't know him to be a smuggler, and it's wrong to judge, particularly beforehand," said the old lady, nursing ideas of rich silks and satins, Imported free of duty and sold at half price, and trying to deceive herself. While she was thus thinking, the door opened, and Wool ushered in a stout, jolly-looking tar, dressed in a wide pea-jacket, duck trousers and tarpaulin hat, and carrying in his hand a large pack. He took off his hat and scraped his foot behind aim, and remained standing before the house- keeper, and with his head tied up in a red bandanna hankerchief, and his chin sunken in a red comforter that was wound around his throat. "Sit down, my good man, and rest while you show me the goods," said Mrs. Condiment, who, whether he were smuggler or not, was inclined to show the traveller all lawful kindness. The sailor scraped his foot again, sat down on a low chair, put his hat oft one side, drew the pack before him, untied it, and first displayed a rich, golden-hued fabric, saying: "Now here, ma'am, is a rich China silk I bought in the streets of Shanghai, where the long-legged chickens come from; come, now, I'll ship it off cheap—. " Oh that is a great deal too gay and handsome for an old woman like me," said Mrs. Condiment. "Well, ma'am, perhaps there's young ladies in the fleet? Now this would rig out a smart young craft as gay as a clipper I Better take it ma'am. I'll ship it off cheap." "Wool," said Mrs. Condiment, turning to the servant, "go down to the kitchen and call up the house-servants; perhaps they would like to buy something." As soon as Wool had gone, and the good woman was left alone with the sailor, she stooped and said: "I did not wish to inquire before the servantman, but, my good sir, I do not know whether it is right to buy from you." " Why so, ma'am 7" asked the sailor, with an injured look. "Why, I am afraid—l am very mush afraid you risk your life and liberty in an unlawful trade." "Oh, ma'am, on my soul these things are honestly come by, and you have no right to accuse me I" said the sailer, with a look of subdued indig- nation. "I know I haven't, and meant no harm; but did not these goods pass through the custom-house 7" "Oh, ma'am, now, that's not a fair question I" It is as I suspected, I cannot buy from you, my good friend; I do not judge you; I don't know whether smuggling is right or wrong; but I know that it is unlawful, and I cannot feel free to encourage any man in a traffic in which he risks his life and liberty, poor fellow I" "Oh, ma'am," said the sailor, evidently on the brink of bursting into laughter—" if we risk our lives, sure it's our own business, and you've no scruples on your own account you needn't have any on ours 1" While he was speaking the sound of many shuffling feet was heard along the passage, and the room was soon half-filled with coloured people come In to deal with the sailor. "You may look at these goods; but you must not buy anything." "Lori, mtssus, why 7" asked little Pitapat. "Because I want you to lay out all your money With my friend Mr. Crash, at Tip-top." "But after de good gemman has had de trouble 7" said Pitapat. "He shall have his supper and a mug of ale and go on his journey," said Mrs. Condiment. The sailor arose and scraped his foot behind him In acknowledgment of this kindness, and began to unpack his wares and displayed them all over the floor. And while the servants in wonder and delight examined these treasures and inquired thtir prices, a fresh, young voice was heard carolling along the hall, and the next moment Capitola, in her green riding habit and hat, entered the room. She turned her mischievous grey eyes about, pursed up her lips, and asked Mrs. Condiment if she were about to open a fancy bazaar. "No, my dear Miss Capitola It is a sailor with foreign goods for sale," answered the old lady. "A sailor with foreign goods for sale! umph I yes I I know. Isn't he a smuggler ? " whispered Capitola. "Indeed, I'm afraid so, my dear Tn fact he don't deny it 19 whispered back the matron. "Well, / think it's strange a man that smuggles can't lie!" "Well, I don't know, my dear; maybe he thinks it's no harm to smuggle, and he knows it would be a sin to lie. But where is your uncle, Miss Capitola 7" "Gone around to the stable to blow Jem up for mounting him on a lame horse; he swears Jem shall find another master before to-morrow's sun. But now I want to talk to that bold buccan,:er. Say you, sir! Show we your foreign goods; I'm very fond of smugglers myself!" "You are right, my dear young lady! You would give poor sailors some little chance to turn an honest penny." " Certainly! brave fellows! Show me that splendid fabric Mit shines like cloth of gold." "This, my young lady, is a real, genuine China silk; I bought it myself in my last cruise in the streets of Shanghai, where the long-legged chickens—" "And fast young men come frov I know the place. I ye been all along there I " interrupted Capitola, her grey eyes glittering with mischief.. "This, you will perceive, young lady, is an article that cannot be purchased anywhere except—" "From the manufactory of foreign goods in the city of New York, or from their travelling agents." "Oh, my dear young lady, how you wrong me! This article came from—" "The factory of Messrs. Hoctis & Pocus, corner ot Cant and Come-in street, city of Gotham!" "Oh, my dear young lady—' "Look here, my brave buccaneer, I know all about it. I told you I'd been along there ! " said the girl; and turning to Mrs. Condiment, she said: "See here, my dear, good soul if you want to buy that 'lndia' silk that you are looking at so longingly, you may do it with a safe conscience. True, it never passed through the custom-house— because it was made in New York. I know all about it! Al these 'foreign goods' are manu- factured at the north and qe,nt by agents all over the country. These agents dress and talk like sailors, and assume a mysterious manner on pur-

pose to be suspected of smuggling—because they know well enough fine ladies will buy much quicker and pay much more, if they only fancy they are cheating Uncle Sam, in buying foreign goods from a smuggler at half price!" "So, then, you are not a smuggler, after all !" said Mrs. Condiment, looking almost regretfully at the sailor.

"Why, ma'am, you know I told you you were accusing me wrongfully." "Well, but really, now, there was something about you that looked sort of suspicious." "What did I tell you 1 a look put on on purpose," said Cap. "Well—he knows that if he wanted to pass for a smuggler, it didn't take here," said Mrs. Condi- ment.

"No—that it didn't!" muttered the object of these commentaries.

"Well, my good man, since you are, after all, an honest peddler, just hand me that silk, and don't ask me an unreasonable price for it, because I'm a judge of silks, and I won't pay more than it is worth," said the old lady. "Madam, I leave it to your own conscience. You shall give me just what you think it's worth." " Humph ! that's too fair by half. I begin to think this fellow is worse than he seems 1" said Capitola to herself. After a little hesitation a price was agreed upon, and the dress bought. Then the servants received permission to invest their little change in ribbons, handkerchiefs, tobacco, snuff, or whatever they thought they needed. When the purchases ware all made, and the peddler had done up his diminished pack and replaced his hat upon his head and was preparing LO leave, Mrs. Condiment said: "My good man, it is getting very late, and we do not like to see a traveller leave our house at this hour; pray remain until morning, and then, after an early breakfast, you can pursue year way in safety." "Thank you, kindly, ma'am, but I must be far on ray road to-night," said the peddler. "But, my good man, you are a stranger in this part of the country, and don't know the danger you run," said the housekeeper. "Danger, ma'am, in this quiet country 1" "Oh, dear, yes, my good man, particularly with your valuable pack—oh, my good gracious 1" cried the eld lady, with an appalled look. "Indeed, ma'am, you—you make me sort of un- easy I What danger can there be for a poor, peaceful peddler pursuing his path ?" "Oh, my good soul, may Heaven keep you from .—BLACK DONALD!" "Black Donald—who's he?" "Oh, my good man, he's the awfulest villain that over went =hung!" "Black Donald I Black Donald! never heard that name before in my life! Why is the fellow called Black Donald?" "Oh, sir, he's called Black Donald for his black soul, black deeds, and—and—also, I believe, for his jet black hair and beard." "Oh, my countrymen, what a falling up was there 1" exclaimed Capitola, at this anti-climax. "And how shall I keep from meeting this villain?" asked the peddler. "Oh, sir, how can I tell you? You never can form an idea where he isn't! Only think, he may be In our very midst any time, and we not know it. Why, only yesterday the desperate villain hand- cuffed the very sheriff in the very courtyard! Yet I wonder the sheriff did not know him at once? For my own part, I'm sure / should know Black Donald the minute I clapped my two looking eyes on him !" "Should you, ma'am 7" "Yes, indeed, by his long, black hair and beard! They say it is a half a yard long. Now a man of such a singular appearance as that must be easily recognised 1" "Of course 1 Then you never met this wretch face to face 1" "Me! me! am I standing here alive? Do you suppose I should be standing here if ever I had met that demon? Why, man, I never leave this house, even in the day-time, except with two bull-dogs and a servant, for fear I should meet Black Donald! I know if ever I should meet that demon, I should drop dead with terror. I feel I should 1" "But may be now, ma'am, the man may not be so bad, after all. Even the devil is not so black as he is painted." "The devil may not be, but Black Donald is." "What do you think of this outlaw, young lady 2" asked the peddler, turning to Capitola. "Why, I like him 1" said Cap. "You do?" "Yes, I do! I like men whose very names strike terror into the hearts of commonplace people 1" "Oh, Miss Black 1" exclaimed Mrs. Condiment. "Yes, I do, ma'am. And if Black Donald were only as honest as he is brave, I should quite adore him! so there 1 And if there is one person in the world I long to see, it is Black Donald." "De you really wish to see him ?" asked the peddler, looking intently into the half earnest, half satirical face ot the girl. "Yes, I do wish to see him above all things." "And do you know what happened to the rash girl who wished to see the devil 2" "No—what did 1" "She saw him I" "Oh, if that's all, I dare it and if wishing will bring me the sight of this notorious outlaw, lo! I wish it. I wish it. I wish to see Black Donald," said Capitola. The peddler deliberately arose and put down his pack and his hat; then he suddenly tore off the scarf from his neck and the handkerchief from his head, lifted his chin and shook loose a great, rolling mass of black hair and beard; drew himself up, struck an attitude, called up a look, and ex- claimed: "Behold Black Donald!" With a piercing shriek, Mrs. Condiment swooned and fell to the floor; the poor negroes, men and maids, were struck dumb and motionless with consternation; Capitola gazed for one lost moment in admiration and curiosity; in the meantime Black Donald quickly resumed his disgnise3, took up his pack and wicked out of the room. Capitola was the first to recover her presence of mind; the instinct ot the huntress possessed her; starting forward, she exclaimed: "Pursue him! catch him! come with me! Cowards! will you let a robber and murderer escape " and she ran out and overtook the outlaw in the middle of the hall. With the agile leap of a little terrier she sprang up behind him, seized the thick collar of hi, pea-jacket with both hands, and drawing up her feet, hung there with all her weight, crying: "Help! murder! murder! help! Come to my aid! I've caught Black Donald!" He could have killed her instantly in any one of

a dozen ways I He could have d: iven in her temples with a blow of his sledge-hammer first; he could have broken her neck with the grip of his iron fingers; he only wished t:-) shake her off with- out hurting her—a -difficult task, for there she hung, a dead weight, at the collar of his coat at the back of his neck. "Oh, very well!" he cried, laughing aloud. "Such adhesiveness I never saw! You stick to me like a wife to her husband. So, if you won't let go, I shall have to take you along, that's all So here I go, like Christian with his bundle of sin on his back." And lowing the upper button of his pea-jacket so as to give him more breath, and putting down his peddler's pack to relieve himself as much as possible, the outlaw strode through the hall-door, down the steps, and down the evergreen avenue leading to the woods. Capitola, still clinging to the back of his coat- collar, with her feet drawn up, a dead-weight, and still crying: " Help I murder! I've caught Black Donald, and I'll die bet re I'll let him go." "You're determiewl to be an outlaw's bride, thqt,,s etair Well I've no particular objection.' cried Black Donald, roaring with laughter as he strode on. It was "a thing to see, not hear "—that brave, rash, resolute imp clinging like a terrier, or a crab. or a briar, on to the back of that gigantic ruffian, whom, if she had no strength to stop, she was determined not to release. They had nearly reached the foot of the descent when a great noise and hallooing was heard behind them. It was the negroes who, having recovered from their panic, and armed themselves with guns, pistols, swords, pokers, tongs, and pitch-forks, were now in hot pursuit. And cries of "Black Donald 1 " " Black Donald 1" "Black Donald 1" filled the air. "I've got him! I've got him! help! help! quick! quick I " screamed Capitola, clinging closer than ever. Though still rowing with laughter at the absurdity of his position, Black Donald strode on faster than before and was in a fair way of escape, when lo I suddenly coming up the oath in front cit him, he met— OLD HURRICANE I ( To be continued.)

OUR SHIPS COMPARED WITH THOSE OF OTHER NATIONS.

The ways of the Admiralty are full of mystery, but nothing is more mysterious than the bashful reticence with which that department divulges to the public infor- mation of the most ordinary and commonplace order. As long ago as August 27th, 1895, Sir Charles Mike, in the House of Commonsimoved for a return showing the com- parative strength of the British Empire as compared with the Great Powers in regard to battleships and other war vessels except terpedoes,whether in commission, in reserve,or building and completing for sea. The return seems to have been completed on September 3rd, 1 895 ; but it is only now issued to the public. Perhaps, how- ever, the delay is due to a regard for the appropriate occasion ; and we are bound to confess that the return comes at an apt moment. But its value is somewhat diminished by the fact that, by the confession of the compilers, a large number of the figure as to aggregate revenue, tonnage of mercantile marine,value of seaborne commerce and the like are " conveyed" from the "Statesman's Year Book "and " Lloyd's Universal Re- gister." To these works we also have access, and the sanctity seems to depart from knowledge so acquired. Such as it is, however, the return i convenient, and though we fear lest it may give occasion for many Jingoistic utterances, we give it a hearty welcome. We need not throw up our caps immediately and shout "We've got the ships," but we may take advantage of the information to form a concise idea of our naval strength in comparison with other Powers. Let us see how it stands. In the matter of battleships England stands easily first with twenty-six in commission, sixteen in reserve, and ten building or completing for sea—in all, fifty-two. Let us compare this with Germany and the United States. Germany has twelve in commission, eight in reserve, and three building or completing tor sea, in all. twenty-three. The United States has eight building. So that if we put Germany and the United States togethett we shall have twenty-one more than both, even if Vle imagine all those in the shipbuilding yards are possible factors in any struggle. France is a more serious com- petitor in the naval race. In all, she has thirty-four battleships either ready or preparing. Russia has seven- teen, Italy has fifteen, Austria has eight, and Spain has two. If we take the battleships, both present and pro- spective, of all the Powers together, we have roughly a proportion of two to one against England—ninety-nine belonging to the Powers and fifty-two to England. Under the other headings we compare as follows, taking into account all ships both in commission, in reserve, and preparing for f ea : Coast. Other Defence. Cruisers. Ships. England l7 137 174 France Ruesia lB ... 17 ... 38 Germany Italy Spln Austria United States If then, we compare the totals of all ships, except battleships, we do not come out so very badly compare sion : England France Russia Germany 52 United States 7O Here we are a match in numbers for at least any" three other great Powers. But it must be remembered that we have a large proportion unprepared—fifty four, as against France's twenty-two, Germany's six, Italy'S eleven. and America's eleven. But if our Fleet is thus larger than that of any other two Powers, we must remember that our stake is far larger, and that we are far more vulnerable on the high seas. The aggregate tonnage ofour mercantile marine is hugely out of proportion to that of any other nation: England France 1,091,752 Spain Russia 487,681 Austria Germany 1,886,812 United States Altogether, the return is very interesting and suggeS: tive, and though it will reassure anyone who may haV" fears as to our strength on the seas, it will breed wholesome caution in those who are here reminded 0 the tremendous istues at stake for us, as a Naval Power• in any combat for the supremacy of the ocean—tho highway of our commerce and our food.

HAPPIEST OF ALL.

THERE iS no time in the twenty-four hours when one ought to feel so thoroughly satisfied and content as

immediately after a good hearty meal. And all healthy persons do feel so. The body's demands have been met

and we are as easy and comfortable as though we had paid off an old dun and bad money left. We are access- ible, humane and good-natured. Then, if ever, we will grant a request without grumbling. " True benevol- ence," sass an old crusty friend of mine, "is located in a capable stomach recently filled."

Yes, but what of the incapable stomachs, of which

there are so many 2—stomachs that disappoint and plague their owners, till the act of feeding, so delightful to others, beeomes an act to avoid the necessity of which they are almost willing to die ? Ali, that is quite another thing. These poor souls are they who say, as Miss Wallace says in this letter of hers, I was no longer to be counted among those who laave pleasure in eating. Far from it. As for me I was afraid to eat. I felt the need of food, of course—the weakness and sink-

ing that accompanied abstinence—but what was I to do? The moment I ate, my distress and pain com- menced. No matter how light the repast was, nor how

careful I was not to hurry in taking it, the result was tha same. The distress and gnawing pains • followed, with discomfort in the chest, and a sense of:choking, as if soms:bits of:food had lodged:there and were irritating me.

"So objectionable and repugnant to me was the act of eating that for days together I did'nt touch a morsel of solid food, subsisting entirely on milk and soda water. Owing:to this enforced lack of nourishment I got extremely weak, and about as thin as I could be. I must not forget to say that this happened to rn.3, or rather it began to happen in July, 1886, when I was living at Wellington, in Shropshire. It;came on, as you

may say, gradually and not with any sudden or acute symptoms. I found myself low, languid, and tired. Then came the failure of my appetite and the other things I have named.

" I took the usual medicines forlindigestion, but they had no good effect. After six months experience of this kind of misery I iread in a book about Mother Seigel's Syrup as a remedy for this disease, ,and got a bottle from Mr. Bates the chemist, in Wellington. Hav- ing used it a few days I felt great relief, and when I had consumed two bottles I was entirely well. Since then I have heartily commended Mother Seigel's Syrup, to many friends, who have,invariably been cured as I was. You have my permission to publish my letter, if you desire to do so. Nigned) Minnie Wallace, Nurse, The Union Workhouse, Oldham, February 22nd, 1895.

Ins communication dated January Bth, 1895, Mrs. Henrietta McCallam of 40, Downsfield Road, Waltham- stow,near London,states that her daughter Emma fell ill in the spring of 1886 with the same symptoms described by Miss Wallace. She:craved food, yet, when it was placed before her she turned from it almost with loath- ing. "As time:went:on," so runs the mother's letter, "my daughterOpecame so weak she could hardly walk. Neither home medicines:nor those of the doctors did any good. Her sufferings continuedlor over eight years. "In June,. 1894, she began taking Mother Seigel's Syrup' of which we had:just read in a little book that was left atithe house. In a:week she was better and in less than two months she was 'enjoying better health than ever before. She has since ailed nothing, and can eat any kind of food. (Signed) (Mrs.) Henrietta IMcCallam."

"Happy," sings Homer" were they who fell under the high walls of,Troy." Happier are they who have never fallen under the crushing weight of indigestion or dyspepsia.!Happiest,perhaps,are all they who have been lifted up by Mother Seigel's remedy and placed where once again they can eat, drink, and be merry. And if all:these could be gathered together they would make a greater host than the Greek poet ever dreamed of.

ANCIENT GEOGRAPHY.

The world to the ancient and medireval inhabitants appeared a far different thing than it seems to the well informed man. of to-day. As to the shape of the earth the ancients differed. Of course in extreme antiquity, every one, who thought about the matter at all,believed the world to be flat. Homer evidently regarded it as of comparatively small extent, and he thought the ocean was a mighty stream which flowed around it. Of course in his time, the earth was supposed to be the centre of the universe, and the sun, the moon and the stars were supposed to be large balls of fire, a few miles up in the air, attached, in some mysterious manner, to the walls of the sky,which walla tevolved around the earth taking the stars with them. The universe was a very small affair, which the gods,who dwelt upon the top of Mount Olympus—by no means a very lofty mountain—ruled over as they thought best. The gods themselves were not very exalted beings, and had disgraceful rows among themselves, and did what seems to-day like some very silly and impudent things. A few hundred years later, in the time of Aristole, a more correct, but a very far from perfect idea of the world had been ob- tained. Aristotle thought the world was spherical ,although several ancient philosophers who eucceeded him did not agree with him. But very little was known in regard to the extent of the world in Aristotle's day,in the fourth century before Christ, and but .a very little more was known about it 1800 years later , in the time of Columbus. In 2,000 years the world had in reality retrograded rather than advanced. It was a popular belief in the time of Columbus that the world was flat,though many contemporary scholars thought differently. The great civilisations of the world at that time were grouped around the Mediterranean Sea, although England was a considerable power, and the Scandinavians were a great maritime people. But Europeans, at that time, knew but little of Asia,and but little of Africa ; and America, of course,was undreamed of. Even after Columbus had discovered the latter con- tinent he was perfectly oblivious of the fact. He thought Hayti was Cipango, or Japan, and for a long time re- garded Cuba as a part of the mainland of Asia.Medireval Europe knew but very little of Eastern and Nerth-east- ern Asia. Many of the most learned cosmographers of the time taught that Asia stretched eastward indefi- nitely, and no one imagined that it had an eastern coast by the ocean. It was seriously taught that Eastern Asia wag a land of vast swamps, inhabited by monster ser- pents and dragons. This was the opinion that still pre- vailed up to within 200 years of the time of Columbus. At this time two Venetian merchants, by the name of Polo, went on a vast trading expedition to the utter- most parts of Asia. They were gone many years. Upon their return the son of one of them,a young man named Marco Polo, wrote out a full account of their travels. described he empire of the Grand Khan—the Chinese Emperor—and revealed the fact that Asia was bounded on the east by a vast ocean. He described this eastern coast minutely, with all its vast cities and its wealth of precious stones and spices. It was from reading this book that the imagination of Columbus was fired,and he conceived the bold conception of reaching this eastern coast of Asia by sailing toward the west,around the earth. So when he discovered Cuba he had not a deubt that he had landed upon the coast of Asia.and that he looked upon the same scenes that Marco Polo had gazed upon 200 years before. Africa was an unknown continent, as well as Asia. Until a few years after the great dis- covery of Columbus no one had sailed around it, and it was quite generally throught, as it had previously been thought in regard to Asia,that the African Continent ex- tended on and on indefinitely. It was supposed that far to the south was the Zone of Heat, in which no which no human being. could live. This was by no means an unreasonable inference to the ancients. They knew that the further they sailed to the north the colder it grew, and that in the extreme north was a region of perpetual ice and snow. They also knew that the further they sailed to the south the warmer it grew ; and what was more natural than for them to suppose that, if they went far enough in that direction, they would come upon lands that were parched and banked, and upon seas that boiled,where nothing could live but salamanders. The ancients peopled all distant lands with monsters and all distant seas with horrors. Un- known and distant countries were, to their superstition, peopled with" gorgoees, hydras, and chimeras dire." The distant seas were filled with monstrous dragons and serpents, of which the sea-serpent is to-day, perhaps, a survival. The unknown oceans were filled with whirl- pools that sucked in mariners, who were sacrilegious enough to approach them, to an awful death. Perhaps these superstitions, as much as any one thing, kept the world for so many centuries an unexplored and un- known waste.

Til EXAMINER, SATITRDAY, JANUARY 25, 1896.

A WELLKNOWN SWIMMER SUMMONED AT THE ST. HELENS POLICE COURT.

It)bert Sharrock, the well-kwown amateur swimmer, was sun-.moned at St. Helens on Monday on the charge of assaulting his wife, Alice Sharrock. Mr. H. L. Riley appeared for complainant, and Mr. J. P. Mearns de- fended. The ages of the parties were given as twenty and nineteen, and they were married six weeks since.— Mr. Riley stated that the case was a deplorable one, for during the whole six weeks of the married life of this young couple the wife had been subjected to a course of persistent cruelty. He had, therefore, to ask their wor- ships to act upon the fourth section of the new Act with reference to the relations of women and their husbands. The first assault took place three weeks ago, but since then it had been aggravated bv more recent conduct, and finally, on the 15th inst., he assaulted her. Defend- ant worked at one of the collieries of Messrs. R. Evans and Co., and earned from 163. to 425. a week. He was also a distinguished swimmer, and added to his income in that way.—Mr. Mearns: He is not a professional swimmer.—Mrs. Sharrock was then called. She stated that she was married on the Monday and on the follow- ing Friday her husband stayed out until 2.30. She asked him where he had been, and he said that had nothing to do with her; he had been about his own business. He also said that if she did not mind he would throw her out. On another occasion he told her he had been in bad company. Three weeks ago she was standing by the fireside and he struck her a violent blow, and she fell and hurt her side. She was led home, and attended by Dr. O'Keeffe for four days. On the 15th inst. he returned from work about half-past four, and, having had his dinner, he went to his mother's and returned about seven o'clock. Her mother had lent her a lot of things for the house, and he began to pull down the pictures and bed, arid told her to send for her mother to fetch them. She did so ; . her mother came, and he threw thim through the window to her. Complainant was ordered out, but refused to go. He lay on the sofa, and she sat on the fender for a while. He spat in her face, struck her in the breast, and turned her out with violence. She then went to her mother's.—By Mr. Mearns : She was not ashamed to come to court and ask for a separation. She was not ashamed of having married him, and if he had behaved himself it would have been all right. She had been a barmaid in Warrington, and had a comfortable life, but she had not said she wanted to leave him and go back. She would live with him, but dare not. She had only known him four months before marriage.— Dr. O'Keeffe said complainant suffered from general de- bility on account of her husband's violence.—Mr.Mearns submitted that the defendant was the aggrieved party, that his wife continually left him, had stripped his house and left him with only a bed and chair. Mrs. Sharrock seemed to have tired of married life in a very short space of time.—Constable Jackson said he saw Mrs. Owen, the mother-in-law, strike Sharrock on the night ef the assault.—Sharrock was bound over to keep the peace for three months.— Elie magistrates added that application for a separation order would not be con- sidered that day. It mnst be made in the ordinary way.

RAILWAY SPEED.

I see no reason why we may not expect to double the speed of steam-driven railroad trains, writes Mr. Hiram Maxim, in " Cassell's Magazine." Ordinary electric trains should travel at the rate of ninety to one hundred miles an hour, and express trains at, say, one hundred and twenty ; but in order to do this it would be neces- sary to so construct the carriages as to enable them to pass through the air without any great resistance. The I train should be pointed at both ends, and have the ap- pearance of all being ir one piece ; even the wheels and axletrees would have to be boxed in. I find in my ex- I periments that atmospheric skin triction on a smooth surface is so very small that it need not be considered as a factor at all, but the power required to drive a rough or irregular body through the air is very great. Electricity could of course, be advantas,,eously employed on exist- ing roads, but if special roads were to be constructed a comparatively cheap line could 1351 employed, and as the electrical train would be vastly lighter than the steam train, extensive grading and tunnelling would nct be neeessary. The line might follow, apofc_zimately, the contour of the country. In the steam-driven train great power is required to enable it to mount even a slight gradient, and all this energy is wasted in heat and friction en the brakes in descending the next grade. The extra amount of energy consumed by an electrically driven train in mounting a , gradient could again be utilised in descending the next I gradient, because the descending train, moving at a high velocity, instead of having its speed checked by the use of brakes, could turn as witch in such a direction as to convert the motors themselves into generators, which would aetually send a current into the line which would be available for the use of other trains.

A BOTANICAL CLOCK.

Among the curiosities which have been found in the Isthmus of Tehuantepec, lately explored by naturalists, is a botanical clock. It is a flower, somewhat resembl- ing in its habits the chameleon, although more methodi- cal. In the morning it is white, at noon it is red, and at night blue, and the alterations of colour are so regular that the time of the day can be told from the tint of the flower. It has been suggested that if it could be accli- matised in a temperate zone, it would be a very useful flower in public parks.

THE MORPHIA. FIEND, STORY OF AN ESCAPR

Few things are more pleasing to the eye than a man of fine health and magnificent stature. A type of such (writes a Liverpool reporter) is to be found in Mr. Joseph Lee,of 26, Pearson-street, Wavertree,Liverpoool. Yet, as it appears, Mr. Lee had a narrow escape from the opium eater's fate. "You have ceme to see me about my remarkable escape, have you 2" he asked, on being visited by our cerrespondent. The latter assented. " Well," said Mr. Lee, " I can tell you outright that it is a true ' escape,' if, as we are always being told, morphia, even when properly used (and in my case it was undoubtedly used with the greatest medical and scientific propriety), often enslaves the patient and transforms him into a regular opium-eater."

" How came you to use morphia, Mr. Lee? ' " On account of the torture I suffered from my late illness. My doctor was a most skilful medical man, and I owe great gratitude to his attendance." " Your illness being what, Mr. Lee ?" "Sciatica. The doctor said I must have caught a " When did you have the first attack 2"

"Four years ago. I endured the most terrible agony for months, and had to remain in bed, during which time I used morphia on thirty occasions—the only thing which gave me relief." _ __

"That's a good deal of morpbia, Mr. Lee.''

" Yes ; and the doctor said that there is probably not another man in Wavertree who could have taken one third of that amount. But the illness left me so terribly weak, and when I recovered I made up my mind not to use mcrphia again. I didn't want another attack but it came on again about the beginning of this year. I was only just able to crawl about, with terrible tortar- IGg pains in my hips both night and day. I used every- thing that I could think of. You can tell what I was suffering when I say that up to August I had lost two stone in weight! One day in that month a friend of mine said to me, Look here, Lee; you try a box of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, and give them a fair trial.' As a last resource I did so. And I never did a better thing in my life. Before I had finished the first box I found myself mending. I got a second one, and I could feel myself going better by bounds every day, and before I had finished half of the third box I was com- pletely well." Mrs. Lee enthusiastically attested the remarkable effect Dr. Williams' Pink Pills had had upon her husband. They were famous as a special tonic, and give strength to the entire system, thus curing sciatica, rheumatism, neuralgia, paralysis, Locomotor Ataxy, nervous head- ache, St. Vitus' dance and Influenz% ; while at the same time they enrich the blood, and thus cure Ansernia, scrofula, chronic erysipelas and the like,and restore pale and sallow complexions to the glow of health. They are obtainable of all chemists, and by post from Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, 46. A specific for all ladies' troubles, and in men cure the ills arising from worry, fatigue, and excess, whether of work or otherwisp.

Steps are being taken to found a memorial of Sergius Stepniak, and the form proposed is a fund to provide for his widow, who has been left desti- tute by the tragic accident which ended the exile's life. The committee appeal to friends of liberty especially in England and America,to all those who believe in the need and possibility of social and economic regeneration, and to all who have read Stepniak's books and who have thereby learnt something of the force and power of his work.

STOCKTON HEATH.

APPLIMON PAROCHIAL COMMITTER.—A meeting of this committee WAS held at the London Bridge Inn, Stockton Heath, on Friday last. Mr. W. Richardson occupied the chair, and there were also present :—Messrs. J. Whitlow, C. W. Davenport, Coppock, Ashton Hayes, Hedgecock, J. Ashton (clerk), J. Swinton (surveyor), J. Farrington (inspector of nuisances). A letter was read from Mr. J. Lyon Whittle, town clerk of Warrington, stating that the suggestions of the committee relating to the proposed new sewer had been agreed to. He stated that the proper course for the committee would be to submit the plan and report to the Local Government Board. The Clerk stated that he had written to Mr. Potts, clerk to the County Council, explaining how the matter stood.—Mr. Marshall Stevens, of the Manchester Ship Canal Company in acknowledging the receipt of a letter from the committee with reference to the sanding of the bridges over the company's canal in their district, stated that he had given instructions for the same to be sanded whenever there was any frost, without, how- ever, the company expecting any responsibility in the matter. A report of the committee appointed to consider the question of lighting streets at Stockton Quay was read,in which they recommended that two gas lamps be fixed, one at the junction of Chapel-lane with Cawdor- st., and the other at the junction of Bostock-st., with Bedford-st., but inasmuch as the latter lamp would con- siderably light several of the trustee's streets, the com- mittee did not approve of lighting the trustee's streets further until they were open for vehicular traffic. The report was received and adopted, and the Clerk was in- structed to request the Corporation ot Warrington to fix the lamp posts. A suggestion made at the meeting of the Runcorn Council with reference to the readjustment of the 'duties of the inspector of nuisances and the sur- veyors of highways was submitted, and it was resolved that subject to its being a stipulation that the Inspector of Nuisances for the Budworth sub-district reside in the township of Appleton, the sukgestion of the Rural Council be approved.—The minutes came before the Runcorn Rural Council at their meeting on Tuesday,and were confirmed.

WYCLIFFE P.S.A. TOTAL ABSTINENCE

SOCIETY.

INAUGURAL MEETING.

On Monlay evening the first meeting of the total abstinence society which has been formed in connection with Wycliffe Congregational Church P.S.A., was held in Wycliffe Hall, the Rev. John Yonge presiding. Councillor Roberts and the Rev. F. G. West (Liverpool), accompanied the chairman on the platform. There was a good attendance. It might be mentioned that the Rev. John Yonge is president of the new association, and Councillor Roberts a vice-president. The meeting commenced by singing and prayer after which The CHAIRMAN said it was with feelings of unusual pleasure that he took the position of chair- man in connection with that meeting. He had be- lieved for a very considerable time now that they needed in connection with the P.S.A. Society, some means of bringing their brethren together on a week night. Their meetings on a Sunday afternoon as they all knew had been crowned with great success, and had awakened a very widespread interest, which had been well main- tained for nearly three years. (Applause.) They also be- lieved a great deal of good had been done to the men who had been banded togetheein the P.S. A.ln connection with the latter movement from starting,there was a tempor- ance committee appointed, and a temperance pledge book was kept in readiness so that any men who wished to sign the pledge could do so, and in the Sunday afternoon meetings some 50 or 60 men had signed the pledge since the P.S.A. started, and the speaker was hoping that they all or nearly all had been able to main- tain their pledges. He had felt strongly that they needed some method of enabling these men who thus took the pledge and became total abstainers to meet together sometimes with friends who sympath- ised with the movement, in order that they might be strengthened in their new faith and practice. In order to do this they decided to have in connection with the P.S.A. some means by which their brethren might have social enjoyment as well as maintain the pledge they had taken. They therefore started a total abstinence society, of which this was the inaugural meet- ing. Their purpose was—and he hoped to be able to carry it out—to have a meeting once every Monday night,in some room on the school premises. They trusted to have once a month an open meeting like the present one and for which they would get special attractions,and by those means support the new movement in connec- tion with the P.S.A. Mr.Yonge was glad to see the !Ales as well as the gentlemen present that night—(hear.hear, and applause)—because, although their P.S.A. for men only had been so wonderfully successful, many of them bad felt that the women were a little bit overlooked by that movement. In some towns it had been found suc- cessful to have Pleasant Monday Evenings for women only,and they had been found evenlmore successful than those for the men only. They had nothing of that kind here,but it was tl eir purpose that the temperance meet- ings here should be open to men and women, and to see "both sides of the House" represented. He had a firm conviction that the old saying was perfectly true that "a man is what a woman makes him," and that conse- vently it was well to have wives and sisters and sweet- hearts on the side of temperance to keep the man true. The idea was that the membership should be a penny a week and he hopu I they would have a consi- derable number of men and women joining the society that night or shortly, and that they would have a series of interesting meetings and that much rood might be done. It was time something more energetic was done in the temperance cause. (Hear, hear.) They were not ambitious, but they were earnest,and he believed that if alllwould help and by God's blessing the Total Abstinence Society would be effectual in doing a great deal of good. He concluded by urging them te try and make Warring- , ton a steady, sober, moral. and religious tnwn, and if all , did what they could and by Divine help they would accomplish it. The Rev. F. G. WEST expressed the pleasure it gave him to see such a good gathering, and he thought it L spoke well for the future of the movement. They should all stand by Mr. Yonge in this matter and if they did— he did not profess to be a prophet—but he would pro- phesy that they should have grand times. For two years at Byrom Hill. Liverpool, they had kept together a meeting of 200 each week. (Hear, hear and applause.) A remarkable fact also was that they had five managers from Walker's public-houses who were members of their church and who had given up the drink trade and taken shops. (Applause.) Those men were convinced of the evils of drink, and had found out the blessings of tem- perance. Mr. West then launched into an excellent dis- course based upon the heads: "Drunkenness is ruin- ous," "Moderation is dangerous," "Total abstinence is safe."

Mr. THOMAS JonNsow, of the Manchester Ragged School, gave an earnest and encouraging address Mug- trating the evils of intemperance and the benefits of total abstinence. He urged all, especially young men to be brave and true in the right cause. Councillor Roamers said he did not desire to make a long speech because he thought the remarks of the pre- vious speakers should be allowed ti' have full weight, and he himself hoped to be "able to do some more prac- tical work later on in the session just commenced. He must say however that he would like to emphasise the appeals made by Mr. West and Mr. Johrson. (Hear, hear.) He did not think there was any logical reply to the speeches to which they had listened, but those of them who were teetolalers had frequently met with this difficulty : If they had tried to induce some of their friends to give up the habit of taking strong drink they had been met with the question, "Why should I be a teetotaler,?" but the speaker had never been able yet to meet the man or woman who could give a Ruud sub- stantial reason for not being. It was the personal liking for the thing which had become the habit fixed in the lives or some reople. He (the speaker) was not hypo- critical in this matter and did not ask a man to do what he did not do himself. (Hear, hear.) All his life, and he had now seen 42 years, he had been a teetotaler, and he was not aware that he was any worse for it but he was sure he was a great deal better for it. (Hear, hear and applause. 1 All of them, he said, who were fathers, should ha careful to tread in the right path for the benefit of the children coming behind, because in their hands would be the powers of England in.the near future. The sacrifice was so slight and the advantages gained would be so enor- mous that during the next 10 years in this country the results would be such that those of the most prophetic amongst them could not possibly contemplate. Mr. Roberts added his personal appeal to thnee who had not yet made up their minds, to come and join with them in their fight and they would try and make Warrington, which they all loved so much, a little better for the fact that they had lived in it. (Loud applause.) Mr. G. Tnonirson, Sankey, said he would be glad to come to their meetings as often as possible. He had been a teetotaller 30 years and he assured all of his sympathy and expressed his willingness to give all the assistance he could.

During the evening Miss Broadhurst sang " Killarney " and was loudly applauded. Mr. J. Taylor. Earlestown, gave an amusing Lancashire reading. entitled" Ringlev Broth," and a song " Go child and beg a bit of bread" was rendered with accordion accompaniment, by Mr. Nightingale. The CHAIRMAN on behalf of the meeting thanked the speakers and singers for their help. Re considered the meeting that night a very auspicious gathering. After the singing of the Doxology the gathering dispersed.

MIRACULOUS CURES OF ASTHMA.

The statements which we publish below confirm the claim of Dr. Schiffmann that he has now discovered an absolute remedy for Asthma, a disease which has hither- to baffled the skill of the most noted physicians. Jonx WILLIAM LOWE, Hopkinson's Yard, Chesterfield, Derby- shire, says :—" For two years I was an incessant sufferer from Asthma, and the winter before last I was unable to work at all, but after using two boxes of your Asthma Cure I was quite cured. Last winter I did not lose a day's werk, nor did I suffer from a single attack.
