A PARADISE FOR RAILWAY EIIPLOYES.

New South Wales in the happy land of the railway employes. It appears from the report of the govern- ment railways in this colony that locomotive engine drivers get lls. to 15s. a day, firemen Bs. to 109., porters 69. to 125., and other classes in proportion. The pay of stationmasters varies from £l5O to £4OO per annum. The railway servants also enjoy incidental advantages. Every servant, except salaried officers, is granted a day's leave on full pay for each proclaimed public holiday (averag- ing about twelve days each year). Engine drivers, firemen, guards, signalmen, and gangers are allowed six good conduct holidays ; expenses, averaging 39. per day are also allowed to drivers, firemen, guards, and all others when away from their home station. Lodging houses with attendants, moreover, are also provided at all the principal depots for drivers, firemen and guards, free of charge ; stationmasters and officers in charge enjoy free houses; the salaried staff get three weeks' holiday per annum on full pay; and lastly, free passes, "station to station," are allowed, when on leave, to each member of the staff, including wife and family, not exceeding three persons.

===== 0002601_18960125_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SATURDAY EVENING CONCERT.

The Parr Hall was again crowded on Saturday night when another of tbe popular concerts was given. A novelty in the programme was the pre- sence of the Ladies' Euterpean Orchestra,conducted by the Misses Scott, of Liverpool, and which com- prises nine performers. The entertainment was commenced with an overture from the "Bohemian Girl," and an intermezzo "Loin dul Bal," both of which were played with taste and precision. Other items provided by these ladies were " Danse Styrienne " (Michiels), " Faust" (Reviere), "Dream of Spring" (011erenshaw), "Baby's Sweetheart," and the 'Gondoliers." " Baby's Sweetheart" was chiefly distinguished by prettiness and simplicity, while the " Gondoliers " was preferred to the re- 'mainder of the selections. Miss Annie Scott was leader of the troupe, and her violin solo Trovatore " was played in a finished manner. A mandoline solo " Cardova " (Sac- chi) was a fine performance, and reflected credit on Miss Greta Scott, the player. Miss Emilie Scott, A.P., T.C.L., was warmly applauded for her piano- forte rendition of "Balmoral," and the" Havelock March " was as well received when given on the banjo by Mr. Guthrie Scott. Mr. J. M. Bate (War- rington), sangthe " Death of Nelson," with consid- erable expression, but there was an improvement manifested in his rend-tion of " Mary of Argyle." Another local artiste was Miss Eleanor Pickton.who greatly pleased her audience by showing not only. the possession of a soprano voice of high range but also a complete command of it. For her singing of the "Children's Home," the audiensle demanded bar re-appearance three times,and later on in the even- ing she met with as hearty approval of her rendi- tion of the " Flight of Ages," for which she gave as an encore, "The Better Land." As humorist the Corporation had secured the services of Mr. William Pagan, who pleased only a section of the audience. It is, of course,not an easy matter for promoters of concerts to provide humorous talent of the highest order every week.

SATTIRDAY POPULAR CONCERT.—A concert WRS given in the Wesleyan Schoolroom on Saturday when Mr.Dodd presided over a crowded audience. Mr. W. A. Wood ' opened the programme with a pianoforte solo (" Valse de Concert ") played in excellent style. Miss Simkin. of Runcorn, sang with much taste" Alone on the raft." Mr. F. Fieldhouse followed with" Anchored" (encored), and he afterwards sang" To-morrow will be Friday." The humorist. Mr. " Teddy" Whittle, of Bolton, contri- buted four items to the programme; two humorous sketches, "The Village Concert" and " I wish I were younger again ;" a humorous song, " Sit thi deswn," and a recital entitled " Franky's Dream." Mr.Whittle's wit and humour carried the audience by storm, and he also showed in his last contribution that he possesses pathos to an unusual degree. Miss May Clare, of Earles- town, charmed all present with the compass and beauty of her voice in her song," Sing, Sweet Bird," In response to an enthusiastic encore she sang" Annie Laurie." She afterwards gave "When the tide comes in," and with Miss Simkin, the duet, "Over the Hawthorn Hedge." Mr. T. Truslove pleased many with his burlesque Imi- tations of cornet solos. The National Anthem brought a most successful concert to a close.

WORDS FROM AN M.P.

Many public speakers wonder how J. Havelock- Wilson, M.P., the Labour member for Middlesboro', could make so many speeches as he did during the last General Election without breaking down in health. He is one of the most untiring public speakers in the Empire, and lam sure many will be interested in the following letter :

After being under the attention of her medical alviser for three months, he recommended your Emulsion, from which she derived much benefit, and I have myself for the past ten years used your Emulsion constantly. WARRINGTON WOMEN'S LIBERAL ASSOCIATION.

ANNUAL MEETING.

The annual meeting of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association was held at the Liberal Club, Bewsey-road, on Monday evening, when the chair was occupied by Councillor William Pierpoint, J.P. There was a good attendance, including Mrs. Handel Booth, of Eccles, Mrs. Z. Armitage, Mrs. H. Roberts, Mrs. W. Pierpoint, Mrs. R. Garnett, Mrs. J. Yonge, Miss Bessie rs. G. Clay, Mrs. W. Furness, Miss Garner, Mrs. Dutton,dolford, Mrs. Cook, Councillors Plumpton and Tinnion, and Mr. C. Jackson Holmes. Councillor PIERPOINT said that the position in which he was placed that night was one which to him was of considerable novelty, because it was the first time he was privileged to preside over any meeting of a _ .

women's Associalion. And they knew that men as a

rule felt rather nervous should they have to address a business meeting of women, because after all, he must admit that women were not going to be led astray by fmthy words so easily as men were. (Laughter.) When, however, he was asked to preside over the annual meet- ing of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association, he felt that Use least he could do was at once to accept that position and endeavour as quietly and as briefly as he could to introduce the business they were called upon to engage in. He was bound to admit that the Women's Liberal Association had done some very good work in Warrington. It had done a great deal more and better work than a great many people outside imagined it was possible it could do. (Applause.) He was in a position to be able to testify the fact that they rendered useful and beneficial work. They would recollect that the men's Liberal Association of Warrington had become extravagant and that they managed their affairs so badly that they actually got on the verge of bankruptcy and hardly knew how to turn or where to look for assistance which they felt was absolutely necessary. They thought that they would have to go" spark out." A great many ladies of Warrington were made aware of the fact and they all put their shoulders to the wheel,he was going to say manfully—(laughter)—but he would say with true womanly courage, and decided that they would on behalf of the Warrington Liberal Association, carry out a bazaar. (Applause.) He need not remind anybody in that room of the phenomenal success which that bazaar was. It was not only pleasant from a social point of view, and perfectly satisfactory to anybody concerned, but the financial result of the bazaar relieved the Warrington Liberal Association of a great weight of debt which was hanging round its neck. (Applause.) The men again went on in a very bungling manner— (laughter)—and they again found themselves in diffi- culties.He hoped he was not disclosing any secrets of the but they again appealed to the Warrington Women's party Liberal Association, and they organised, not a bazaar,because their wants were not very great,but a sale of work and got for the Association,the further sum of £55. Altogether, the bazaar and sale of work realised the very handsome sum of something between £BOO and £l,OOO. (Applause.) If the women of Warrington had been able to do that on behalf of Liberal politics it had been of great use. (Hear, hear.) He was afraid he was again dis- closing secrets, when he said that they still looked upon some of the good ladies of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association again to help them out of one ot those little and slight financial difficulties into which they seem to fall so easily. If they had been of such great use in the past, it was very clear to him that they could be of very great use in the future. He was not going to say that they were only of use id finding money, but they could be of very great political use by their example and by their constant advocacy of Liberal principles which after all were for the greatest good of the greatest number. (Applause.) He was glad that one of the active members of the Association, Mrs. Clay, had ventured to take her part in public life—(applause) —and she had not only earned the gratitude of the party, but of all liberal-minded and good people in Warrington. (Applause.) If they had only done that, they had earned the gratitude of all good Liberals. He was sure that Mrs. Clay would not mind if there were another,or perhaps four or five lady Guardians along with her. (Applause.) Mrs. PIERPOINT, in the absence of Miss BISCRALL, then read the following report :—" The Warrington Women's Liberal Association was inaugurated at the suggestion of the Countess of Carlisle in 1891, under the presidency of Mrs. Ziba Armitage, and has been quietly and steadily working until there is every prospect of its becoming s powerful organization, a means of spreading true Liberal principles, and of educating women to take an intelli- gent interest in politics, and to consider the many im- portant questions brought before Parliament. In the spring of 1892 Mrs. (now Lady) Fry,of Darlington,came over and addressed a crowded women's meeting at the Liberal Club, Bewsey-street, Mrs. Crosfield, Walton Lea, being in the chair. At the conclusion many new mem- bers were admitted. In 1893 a sale of work and concert were held,when Mrs. Stewart Brown.of Liverpool, opened the proceedings. In March, 1894,Miss Southall spoke to a well-attended meeting, and strongly advocated the great necessity for women becoming members of Boards of Guardiars, also for their watching municipal affairs and the class of candidates chosen. Later on a tea party was held; the attendance was more than the spacious room would comfortably accommodate. On this occasion Mr. J.Rylands,J.P..president of the Liberal Association, was in the chair. On November 11th,1895, a small sale of work was held, when Mr. Percival B. Scott, the Liberal candidate for the borough, attended and thanked the members of the Women's- Association for the assistance given to him at the late general elec- tion. The amount realized at this sale was most gratify- ing to the workers. Delegates have been sent each year to the annual meeting of the Women's Liberal Federa- tion held in London. It will thus be seen from this the first report the association has issued that the outlook is most encouraging. The committee invites all Liberal women to join the association.alsoto attend the meetings it is proposed to hold at no distant date,at which speakers from other associations will be present. In conclusion, the President and committee desire to give their best thanks to the members of the Liberal Club for so kindly granting the use of a room for meetings,&c." (Applause.) Continuing, she said that she would be glad if any lady member of the Association had changed her address that they would notify the same to 'Mies Birchall. She honed that if there were any in that room who had not joined that Association, they would do so that night. Her husband had mentioned Mrs. Clay. She had heard that the gentlemen who were members of the Board of Gutrdians had not the slightest objection to having a lady present. One of them had said that he would not mind if there were one or two more if they were only like Mrs. Clay. (Laughter and applause.) Mrs. HENRY ROBERTS then read the balance sheet which showed that the income during the past year had been £127 93. 9d. They had contributed £55 to the Wsr- ringten Liberal Association, and they had still a balance of £37 10s. 7d.

_ The report was adopted on the motion of Mrs. CLtY, seconded by Mrs. COOK. Mrs. HANDEL BOOTH then addressed the meeting. She said that she was exceedingly sorry to hear that the men were in such low water. She must say that that was her experience of all men's Liberal Associations. (Laughter.) The Women's Liberal Association with which she was connected, was getting up a bazaar pre- paratory to getting the men out of debt. She thought that Women's Liberal Associations ought to have origin. ality of ideas. She was afraid that the want of origin- ality was a great fault of them, but she was glad that the Warrington Association had been of some use in re- turning a woman Poor Law Guardian. She hoped that the next time there was an election, they would send her a companion. (Applause). She (Mrs. Booth) had three women colleagues on the Board of which she was a member, and the found plenty of bard work, as they met two and sometimes three times a week. She had been requested by Miis Rirchall to speak that night upon dangers they had before them—the dangerous rocks they had ahead. Certainly it was very difficult at this time to comment upon Tory policy because feeling was still running high. As a very strong &id ardent Radical, she felt bound to protest against they course they had been pursuing. The leaders did not promise much, but the men who were asking for the votes of the working- men all over the country made all kinds of promises. The chief reason she got from workingmen when they said that they were going to vote otherwise than at the precious election was" We mun ha' a change." (Laugh- ter) It was an idea of the Tory party that before Lord Salisbury's august presence, all foreign nations would be very humble and deferentially bow to him. She did not think this had come to pass. (Bear, hear.) They had had a war with Ashantee."She believed it was now over. If they asked her what it was about,she could not tell them, although she had read very carefully, the newspaper reports, and the only reason appeared to be to give some -nembers of the Royal Family an oppor- tunity of having aj little trip. (Laughter.) As to the United States, she did not know whether they had studied the Monroe doctrine. She could not defend Pre- sident Cleveland's actinn,as it was not defended by right minded people in America—(hear, hear,)—and she be- liow-d that the English people had a brotherly feeling towards those of the United States. But the reason that the Venezuela boundary queation had come to the im- portance it had was because Lord Salisbury took some fale information—it mieht not be his fault—and took the Schomhurgk boundary as the absolute boundary. Then there was the oppressed country of Armenia. Fly was present at a large meeting on Sunday week at Manchester , and it seemed to her that all in that vast hall were full of sympathy but they all seemed to feel a sense of impotency, and in not being I able to help them. England seemed only able to give the sufferers our sympathy in word. She did not know whether the Government was acting at present upon j anything of a secret kind,but it seemed to her that Eng- land was indirectly to blame for the massacres that had keen perpetrated in Armenia. As to the probable future Ipolicy of this present Government they had ,she thought, more to fear from it than to hope. There would be ex- travagances upon the navy and army, possible coercion for Ireland again,no relief for those engaged in agricul- ture,and many other promises made by the Tory party at the last election to catch votes would be lift unful- -1'1;1o: They were, however, powerless to interfere while the Tories held ale° ; they would have to allow them to go on in their own stupid course ; but the members of

that association should in the meantime endeavour to their utmost to gain the franchise. Then they would be able to give their support to that greater Liberalism in a better and truer manner than did the men at the last election. (Applause.) Miss BESSIE DUTTON said that she was of opinion that any movement carried on exclusively by one sex wag not likely to he complete. Untorliina'ely Mr. Pierpoint had laid a little too much stress she thought upon the work of their hands, and had not thought very much about their heads. (14 iughter.) Tne importance of training the intellectual faculties of women had not always been recognised as it was to-day. Some years ago parents thought if they sent their daughters to a boarding school where these was good air and a dancing master that was all that was required. Then the pre- valent idea was that women had not a mind worth cul- vating, but nnw,fort,:nately, that error had been swept away, and the value of woman's assistance in every branch of work was beginning to be estimated with a near approach to truth. They were accustomed to be dealt out with little compliments, but very little else was given them. (Laughter.) She was of opinion that the vote might be conceded to the womes, and she thoeght that if that were done, the women would know how to use it aright. Women had been very useful in War- rington, as was clearly shown by the report of the association that night. She herself had not been idle, though owing to many other duties she had not been able to do what she would have liked for the association. She hoped the members would not relax their efforts towards influencing the young people with whom they came into contact, to inculcate into their minds a know- ledge of what was right, just and holy. (Applause.) She considered that in the future, religion would be in much closer touch with politics than it was at the present time, and that the question of religion and morality would be much more to the fore. She hoped in con- clusion that everyone there would be encouraged to push forward the good work in which they were engaged and to bring about a better state of things in this country. (Applause.) Mrs. YONGE proposed a vote of thanks to Mrs. Booth and Miss Dutton for their addresses that night. She said that she fully sympathised with women's fran- chise, and thought that they ought to get their rights. She said that she did not want the vote for hcr.elf as long as she had a husband who voted right. (Laughter.) Mrs. GARNETT expressed similar sentiments in second- ing the vote of thanks which was passed unanimously.

A vote of tllanks was also accorded Councillor Pier- point,on the motion of Mrs. ARMITAGE seconded by Mrs. BO@TH.

Councillor PIERPOINT replied, and then suggested that a committee be formed to make arrangements for the prlposed social gathering. He hoped that if one were held, everyone would make it as successful as possible. He would like it to be said that they k,ad held the largest and most successful bazaar which had ever beem held in Warrington, and that they wou1:1 have the largest and best attended social evening which had eve= been organi.ed in Warrington. Mas. PIERPOINT then proposed that a committee be formed, and Mr. C. JACKSON-HOLMES seconded, and the motion was carried.

The following was the committee appointed :—Mra. Armitage, Mrs. H. Roberts, Mrs. Puerpoint,Miss Birchall, Councillor Tinnion,Mr. F. R. Roberts, M.A., L.L.B.,and Mr. C. Jackson Holmes.

This concluded the business of the meeting.

THEY WERE BRAVE MEN, AND THEY SPOKE OUT HONESTLY WHAT THEY BELIEVED.

Dr. Radcliff was the most celebrated physician of England in Queen Anne's time. He amassed a large fortune, and was for many years the physician to the Queen. For some reason the Queen took a dislike to bias at one time, and dismissed him as court physician. In her last illness, however, she sent for him. He refused to obey the summ)ns, declaring: " Since Her Majesty's ease is so desperate, and her distemper incurable, I hardly think it proper to give her any disturbance in her last moments, so near at hand, but rather let her die as easily as possible." _ _ .

Dr. Fisher, Government Physician, Wuerttemburg, Germany, soisie time ago wrote as follows : " When all other therapeutic agencies had failed, Warner's Safe Cure completely cur' t ..an of my patients suffering from Bright's disease disease of the kidneys." Dr. R. A. Gunn, Dean of the United States Medics/ College, and Editor of the" Medical Tribune," some time since wrote and published a book in which he said: "The ingredients of Warner's Safe Cure are among the most valuable medicines of our materia medica, and the combinatimn is such as to insure the best possible action on the kidneys and urinary organs." There are physicians so skilled, so itidependent, so honest, so true to the conscientious teachings of their noble profession, that they dare tell the truth to Kings or Queens, or the whole world. All honour to them.

A correspondent having called Lord Rosebery's atten- tion to the allegation by the Duke of Westminster that he (Lord Rosebery) was trying to make political capital out of the Armenian question, has received a rep!y its which the leader of the Liberal party says be hopes his letters and his career will speak for themselves. Had he wished to make political capital out of the question it would have been easy enough for him to do so. He advises his correspondent to make allowance " for a. Duke in difficulties."

Some sharp passages occurred between Dr. Gordon and prisoner's council during the doctor's ex- amination. Deceased's husband, Herbert Westworth, was committed for trial on the capital charge.

Mc DOUGALL'S PATE yt SELPIt A ISING FLOUR onct Tian ALWAYS USW])

lineage, and traditions, stand as brothers, shoulder to shoulder, in the interests of humanity by a :union compelling peace." Just the evening before that speech was made Rear-Admiral GHERARDI of the United States Navy had spoken in a somewhat similar strain to a gathering of military and naval officers. "The English," said he, "are just as stubborn as our- selves and just as proud. We have inherited these traits from them. I can assure you that England will fight, but the last people whom she wishes to fight are ourselves." This gallant officer's references to the Venezuelans, were not so complimentary. He described them as" a race as despicable and ungrateful as ever lived," and added, "these South Americans do not like us. You cannot trust their word." It may be seen therefore that England is not without her friends and admirers in the United States. They have not lately been so much to the front as those on the other side, but they seem to be coming to the front. It is, in fact, just possible that the sub- stantial benefit of a better understanding may be the result of a disagreement which at one time portended nothing but evil.

ALL who are connected with the The Iron Trade. important iron industry of this country will be interested in c. report which has been prepared on German and Belgian competition. The report is the result of a visit to the countries named by a delegation or- ganised by the British Iron Trade Association. I The delegation consisted of fourteen persons— seven employers and seven representative work- men. Its business was to ascertain the reasons for the steady increase in the exportations of German and Belgian iron to India, to the Colonies, and to Great Britain herself while the home produce was stationary or actually receded. It may at once be said that the delegation has been to a large extent successful in its lahours. It has gathered a great deal of interesting and valuable informa- tion witk regard to the conditions under which the iron industry is carried on in Germany and Belgium. It is able to prove too that if Great Britain is being out-distanced in the race it is due to causes which can, and which should, be removed. What, then, are the advan- tages which the foreigners enjoy P Many people will perhaps be disposed to say that the foreign employer has less wages to pay. But this repprt does not lay any stress on the ques- tion of wages. In Belgium the wages are lower than those paid in England, but then as far as Germany is concerned, and it is getting to be much the more formidable competitor,there is :not the difference of wages that is usually supposed to exist. And in Germany there is not the same extreme of wages—of exorbitantly high payments on the one hand and of correspondingly poor wages on the other. German exployers are also handicapped in a way that English employers are not, for they are compelled by law to subscribe to pension, benefit, and accident funds,sand this:they 'say is a rather serious burden on the iron industry. But,if not in wages, in railway carriage the foreigners have a very commanding advantage. The rates for long distances are about half, or less than half, those the British manufacturer has to pay. A Belgian manufacturer can, for instance, send his iron to Antwerp by rail and thence by sea to London for considerably less than the railway rate from Staffordshire to London. A. still more remarkable instance is given. One of the largest works in Germany situated more than 150 miles from a shipping port, can reach Antwerp by rail for 3s. 6d. per ton of finished iron or steel, whereas similar produce transported from works in the Midlands for a similar distance would be charged about three times as much by the English railway companies. The disparity in this respect is, in fact, so great that the delegation are of opinion that if English manufacturers enjoyed the same railway rates and royalties as those existing on the Continent foreign competition could be defied in neutral markets. In shipping,. too, the British producer is handicapped as " combinations and monopolies which exist among English ship- ping firms have been:used to maintain higher rates of freight from London and Liverpool than are charged for the same service on the Continent." High praise is bestowed upon the equip- ment of the German and Belgian works; the machinery and arrangements being in some cases superior to what can be seen in this country. On the other hand this country has some import- ant advantages in its supply of raw material—coal and iron ore. In Germany coal has often to be carried long distances, and in Belgium the cost of fuel is higher and the quality inferior. On the whole, therefore, it may be said that this report is not by any means hopelessly discouraging. It is clear that the British iron industry can still more than hold its own if certain shackles be removed. Now that the real obstacles which stand in the way of trade expansion have been clearly exposed it may be expected that a strong and united effort will be made to remove them.

DEATH OF PRINCE HENRY OF

BATTENBERG.

We regret to state that Prince Henry of Battenberg, the husband of Princess Beatrice, died at sea on Monday evening last of relapsed African fever. The sad news was entirely unexpected in Court and official circles. When his Royal Highness went on. board her Majesty's ship Blonde , t Cape Coast Castle on Friday last week the attack of fever from which he had then been suffering for eight days was running a normal course. The patient's strength was fairly well maintained, and Surgeon Captain Hilliard, who has accompanied the Prince from Kwisa to the co tat, had not the least cause for objecting to the proposal that his Royal Highness should be sent to Madeira to recuperate. Acting on cabled instructions from London, Surgeon Captain Hilliard himself accom- panied the Prince on board her Majesty's ship Bionde,and took sole charge of the patient. It was confidently expectel that the sea breezes on the voyage and the balmy air of Madeira would speedily restore Prince Henry to health. The Prince was taken on board the Blonde on Friday morning with every possible care and precau- tion, and he did not appear to suffer in any way from the exertion. Soon after leaving Cape Coast Castle,how- ever, the fever assamed a more serious form, complica- tions eventually ensued, and his Royal Highness suc- cumbed quite painlessly an the evening of the 20th. The Blonde at once put back to Sierra Leone, where she arrived on Wednesday morning. From all quarters Princess Beatrice and the Queen continue to receive messages of spmpathy and condo- lence. A bulletin was posted at Osborne on Thursday stating that the Queen and the Princess, " although well in health, are overwhelmed with grief at their sudden and unexpected bereavement." The Blonde, with the body of Prince Henry on board, left Sierra Lorne on Thursday for England: It is expected that she will arrive abut the 7th February. The Prince is to be buried at Windsor, and will be given a military funeral. A special edition of the London " Gazette" on Thursday night announced that the court was to go into mourning until March 5.

GROUNDING OF A STEAMER AT RUNCORN.

On Thursday morning the steamer Gulf of Siam from Brisbane for Manchester grounded in the Ship Canal at Runcorn. Tugs were immediately brought into requisi- tion, and thy succeeded in towing her off after a short delay. She then proceeded to Manchester where she will be examined.

THE latest reports from the other An Improved side of the Atlantic seem to in- Situation. dicate that the worst part of the Jingo fever there is now over. In cases of this kind there is, of course, always the danger of a relapse when the disorder may rage even more virulently than before. But, on the whole, it is only reasonable to expect that no such relapse will take place in the present case. The Amerians have had an opportunity of announcing their Monroe doctrine and they have done so with extraordinary vigour and emphasis, and they are beginning to see that great Britain of allcountries has,and never has had, any intention of treating that doctrine with anything but the utmost respect. They—or at least many of them—are beginning to realise ' too that the danger to peace did not spring from any aggressive purposes which this country had in Venezuela, but rather from the amazingly undip- lomatic menaces of President CLEVELAND himself. The new feeling is being voiced in many ways. Even in the Senate itself a member of the Re- publican party, Mr. WALCOTT, has spoken out in a very pronounced fashion. He denounced Mr. OLNEY'S letter to Mr. BAYARD as "almost in- cendiary," and the President's Message as " glow- ing with the possibilities of war." The Venezuelan Commission he condemns as "more a menace than a guarantee of peace." In the opinion of Senator WALCOTT, President CLEVELAND has been playing with dangerous weapons, for he has appealed to feelings of hostility against Great Britain which have been fostered by many politi- cal leaders in order to distract attention from domestic discontent. This, it seems, has been carried to such an extent that " there exists in the breasts of millions of citizens an antagonism to Great Britian so rampant that all judgment is- unheeded." There can ba no doubt that this feel ing of antagonism to this country exists to a very great extent in the States. It has been spoken of by all sorts of people—by observing and thinking workmen like Mr. H. WHITHAM, whose interesting articles appear from time to time in the "Examiner," and by other writers and speakers who have mixed closely with all sections of the American people. It is the presence of this hostile feeling in the States which made Mr. CLEVELAND'S message a real danger to peace, and it is this feeling that has to be reckoned for in such crises as the present. On the other hand it is quite evident that there is a very large number of Americans whose disposition towards the old country is of quite a different character. For instance, the following fine passage from SENATOR WALCOTT'S speech of Wednesday will be read with unmingled pleasure in this country "When I read" said the eloquent Senator, " that France, Germany, and Russia are allied against Great Bsitain, arid that the people of the British isles, in defence of what they deem their rights, are marshalling armies and assembling navies ready and undaunted to face the world in arms, unyielding and unafraid, I thank God I am of that race. Blood is thicker than water, and until a just quarrel divides us, which heaven forbid, may these two great nations of the same speech

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

THE VOLUNTEER MEDAL. In the list of the fourth award of the Volunteer long service medal, the following are mentioned as recipients in connection with the 22nd V.B. Cheshire :—Private W. Brown, Corporal W. Jones. Privates W. Stretch, T. Woods.

RUNCORN COUNTY COURT.

Ae the sitting OlThis Court on Tuesday before his Honour, Judge Wynne Ffoulkes, there were no disputed cases of public importance. The summary of business is as follows :-7 original summonses, 1 default sum- mons, and 15 judgment summonses. THE BEGGING NUISANCE.

- At Runcorn Police Court on Wednesday morning before Mr. E. Imison, another case of begging was dealt with. The defendant was Thomas Hopkins, and he was committed to Knutsford prison for seven days hard labour for begging in Cawley-lane. HALTON-ROAD WESLEYAN CHURCH, RUNCORN. The annual collections on behalf of the trust funds of this charch,were taken on Sunday. The preachers were —morning, the Rev. C. de P. Gliddon, and evening, the Rev. W. George. The choir rendered special anthems. Miss Clara Taylor gave a very effective rendering of the recitative " Holy Virgin shall conceive," and the solo "Oh thou that tellest." Mr. G. S. Frith presided at the organ.

BETHESDA CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH.

At this church on Sunday on the occasion of the " pleasant evening" service, the pastor (Rev. J. Simp- son) delivered a short address on "Take care of number oae." Miss Simkin gave a beautiful rendering of a solo and Mr. Outhwaite played a selection on the euphonium. The choir gave an excellent interpretation of the anthem " Jean, word of God incarnate," Mr. C. le Couteur pre- siding at the organ. There was a large congregation. COMING OF AGE REJOICINGS. On Tuesday evening a party of upward of forty rela- tives and friends assembled at the residence of Mr. Matthew Sweetman,Urban Bistrict Councillor,to celebrate the coming of age of his daughter, Annie. The proceed- ings were of a most enjoyable character. Numerous toasts were proposed and duly honoured, and vocal and instrumental music was contributed by members of the party. A sub-committee of the Cheshire Standing Joint Com- mittee have under consideration the alteration and en- largement of the police court, &c. It is proposed to pull down the quarters now occupied by the Inspector and to erect on the curve in Mersey-street a new and com- modious courthouse, &c., with a stone front, and con- struct new cells underneath. The present court room will be converted into a parade room for the constables. 'The work is expected to cost some thousands of pounds.

CAMDEN SEWING MEETING. On Thursday evening last, the annual gathering in connection with Camden Wesleyan Mother's Sewing Class was held. when about 100 sat down to tea provided by the President, Mrs. R. Morris. Mrs. Santley in the name of the members presented Mrs. Morris with a splendid edition of the Teacher's Bible. Many ot the members spoke highly of the spiritual good they had re- ceived during the past year, and hoped the President might be long spared to continue her useful work. Mrs. Morris suitably responded, thanking the donors for the kindness extended to her. SERIOUS BURNING ACCIDENT. On Monday evening, a child 6 years of age named Sarah Lizzie Bostock, daughter of Mr. Alan Bastock, Porter-street, Runcorn, received serious burning injuries. It appears that she went to herluncle's house,a few yards away, and whilst warming her hands opposite the fire, her pinafore caught Are and she was burnt abcut the face, throat, and hands. The fire was speedily extin- guished and the child conveyed home. Dr. McKenzie was called in, and is still attending the child who lies in a serious condition.

PLEASANT SATURDAY EVENINGS.

The usual Pleasant Saturday evening entertainment inaugurated by the Runcorn Women's Temperance As- sociation was held in the Brunner Guild Hall. There was a good attendance. The chair was occupied by Mr. T. Reynolds. After devotional exercises by the Rev. W. George, an interesting programme was gone through- Songs were given by Mrs. Stubbs, Misses K. Carter, Wil• son, S. P. Hayes,and Bessie Harrop ; Messrs. T. Charles. ton, and Parry. Mr. Hulse contributed a couple of re- citations, Mr. T. Parry gave a reading, and Mr. A. Povah played two violin solos. The programme was greatly enjoyed. Coffee and buns were dispensed. The meetings so far, have been very encouraging. RUNCORN POLICE COIIRT. _ _

At Runcorn Police Court on Friday morning, before ' Messrs. F. J. Norman and E. Imison, two well-known characters in the persons of Ellen Moran and Margaret Connolly made their appearance for being drunk and disorderly in Mersey-square at midnight. Having been repeatedly convicted, Moran was sent to gaol for a month, and Connelly was ordered to undergo 21 days' incarceration.—On Saturday morning, a sailor named William Coe put in an appearance before Mr. E. Re NCO RN CO-OFR.IIAV VII SOCIETY. On Saturday, the last day for the receipt of nomina- tions by the sec retary of this society for the election of members of the Management Committee, it was found that only eight nominations had been made, and as eight vacancies occurred,there is now no necessity for a. contest as was first anticipated. Therefore at the forth- coming half-yearly meeting the following will be de- clared duly elected :—Runcorn : MeserseToseph Stubbs, Joseph Leadstone, John James Bowyer, and James Moores : Widnes; Messrs. Thomas Cosier, William Wil- iam, George F. Dean, and John Hartles. The treasurer and secretary by the new rules having been appointed permanent officers,the annual meeting will be relieved from their election. Hitherto the nominations have been made at the meetings of the members, and the se- lections have been the result of a show of hands. Now it is laid down that the committee shall consist of 12, that every six months three go out of office by rotation, that nomination papers be obtainable at the various branches, that they be issued 2.1 days before the half- yearly or annual meetings, that they be returned to the secretary within seven days, that the list of nominated persons be posted in the branch establishments, and, if necessary, that the election be conducted by ballot, vot- ing being conducted at the shops at Runcoro, Weston, Frodsham, Widnes, and Appleton. In consequence of another rule to the effect that members having relatives at the stores shall be ineligible, as well as the addition to the committee, there were eight vacancies to be filled on the present occasion. SAD DEATH OF A FOHIERR RUNCORN FOOTBALLER. The remains of the late Mr. Henry Atherton, aged 42 years, a native of Runcorn who died suddenly at Pres- ton Station last Thursday, were interred in the burial ground connected with Bethesda Congregational Church on Sunday afternoon, in the presence of a large com- pany. The deceased who was a memb.r of an old Run- corn family had resided in his native town until about three years ago when he left for America in company with several others to assist in the establishment of the new chemical works there by Messrs. Mathieson, who owned works at Widnes. He returned just before' Christmas on a holiday and resided with his sister at 27, Pembroke-place, Liverpool. On Thursday last when returning from a visi.; to Edinburgh where he had been to see a relative, he suddenly expired at Preston Railway Station. He a'; one time took a very active part in con- nection with the Runcorn Rugby Football Club, and as a splendid forward player he did much to bring the Run- corn club into the prominent position it now occupies. He played in company with such well-known Runcorn players as Messrs. William Wright," Ferry " Bate, Joule, Tudor, Bennett, Hoffiend, Broady and H. Hughes and many lovers of the game will remember his abilities. The funeral possession was jeined in by a number of rela- tives, friends, and past and present playing members of the Runcorn club. The following were the bearers : Messrs. William Wrieht, H. Hughes, G. Wainwright, James Clarke, J. Heffiend, W. H. Broady, H. Andrews. and William Faulkner. Succeeding the coffin were : Alessrs.George and John Atherton (brothers). John Stan- way (brother-in-law), John Starr, Harper, W. Stan way Harry and Frank Atherton (nephews), Robert Merriless (brother-ba-law), J. Jolly, Thomas White, S. Johnson, P. Deakin, James James, E. Hankinson, T. Johnson, and T. Laws (Widnes Football Club.) The Rev. J. S. Simpson was the officiating minister, end he spoke a few words upon the suddenness with tech the deceased had been called away, and urged all to be pre- pared for the Divine call.

FOUND ON THE PREIIISES.

At Runcorn Police Court on Tuesday morning before Mr. James Handley, a lad, 15 years o't age, named Henry Malvern, 56, Cooper-st., was charged with having been found on the premises of Matthew Thomas, 2, New-st., for an unlawtul purpose on the previous day. Mrs. Thomas deposed that on the previous day she locked up the premises about three o'clock, and went to assist at Mr. Hill's heuse in the preparations for a funeral party. Being short of a carving knife, she returned to her house and was surprised to And the door open. She heard someone moving about upstairs and shouted, but got no reply. On going upstairs she found prisoner in the front bedroom, and saw that he had opened several drawers. She asked him what he was doing there, but he ran off. She followed him to an open field at the back of the pre- mises when he called her names and made use of bad language. Information was given to the police and the prisoner was apprehended by P.C. Gregory. He now pleaded guilty, but offered no explanation why he was there. He was committed to prison for five days.

An elderly woman named Mary Plant, I,Penketh-lane, Runcorn, was charged at the Police Court, on Thursday morning before Messrs. E. Imison and S. Taylor, with stealing a child's jacket, value 5a., from Granville-at. Board schools the previous day, belonging to Sarah Douglas, 4, Leinster Gardens.—Florence Douglass a scholar at the schools, gave evidence as to hanging the jacket in one of the classrooms about nine o'clock in the morning of the previous day. She did not see it, again until brought to her by the police. Mrs. Dluelas identified the jacket as her property.—Miss Littler, schoolmistress at Granville-at. school, stated that she was informed by Florenm Douglas that her jacket was missing. She instituted inquiries and found that a woman had been to the school, and information was conveyed to the police.—Thomas Brown, assistant at Lightfoot's pawnshop, stated that the prisoner tried to pledge the jacket for 2s. 6d.—P.C. Gregory apprehended her in the shop with the jacket in her possession.—ln reply to the charge she acknowledged taking it, and stated that it was through her husband who was drink- ing and only worked three days a week. There was no food in the house.—The magistrates said they would take a lenient view of the case, and would send her to Knutsford for seven days without hard labour. RUNCORN FREE LIBRARY LECTURES.

" WORDSWORTH."

In one of the art rooms at Runcorn Technical Institute 1 on Friday evening, another of the lectures promoted by the Runcorn Free Library Committee, was given. Mr. J. J. Ogle, of Bootle, was the lecturer, and the subject was " Wordswoirth." Mr. IV. J. Cunningham occupied the chair, and he was supported on the platform by Messrs. William Handley, S. Pope, T. Riley and J. D. Jones (Librarian). Mr. Ogle in the course of his remarks said there were vary few incidents of a telling character to relate with regard to Wordsworth. His life was very peaceful, aa peaceful as that of our Alfred the Great, the poet laureate, who, he was sorry to say, had now as succevor Alfred the little. (Laughter.) Wordsworth was born it) 1770, and there were many great poets born about that time. Cowper was then hardly in his prime, and after they had read some of Wordsworth's work they would find that Wordsworth was indebted to Cowper for many ideas. After referring to Words- worth's successful college career at Cambridge, he al- luded to the poet's visit to Fran,:e, then in the tumult of excitements which ended in the French revolution. On his return to England in 1792, he settled in Dorsetshire, and afterwards in Somersatslaire, but it was not until 1798, when he was 28 years of age, that he published his most important works. They would find that Words- worth did his most important work between the years 1798-1808. He, however, wrote in such a different strain from that which was fashionable that there were very few who could appreciate his poetry. In 1839, Oxford conferred the degree of doctor upon him, and in 1813 he was appointed poet laureate, a position he held for' nine years,when he was succeeded by Soutney. Many regardeo, Wordsworth as a poet of natural scenery,but if he had not been a poet of men as well as of nature, he would not be one of the greatest poets. He was more Catholic in his treatment of things than most poets. The lecture was it may be added, an intensely interesting one,and many examples of the poet's work were quoted.

SHIP CANAL AND RUNCORN COUNCILS.

THE CLOSING OF TIDAL OPENINGS.:

- When the new Ship Canal Bill was introduced a short time ago, steps were taken by local authorities to ascer- tain how it might affect the neighbourhood if passed by Parliament, It was found that one of the chief points in the Bill was the c!osing of the tidal openings at Elles- mere Port, Weston, and Runcorn. Both the Runcorn Rural and the Urban District Councils have been con- sidering what action to take in the matter, and it has been generally thought that with regard to this applica- tion of the Ship Canal Company, opposition should be made. Negotiations have taken place,and last week the Company offered to send a deputation to confer with members of both Councils. Accordingly on Wednesday both authorities received the deputation at Buncorn. The first conference was with the Runcorn Rural Coun- cil at Bridgewater House when there were present on behalf of the Ship Canal Company,Mr. Bythell (chair- man), Councillor Southern (chairman of the Executive), Sir Leader Williams, Mr. Hunter (engineer for the Canal Company), and Mr. BM (Corporation engineer). The members of the Rural Council present were Messrs. Brandreth, Heywood, Chadwick, Aaron, Wallington, Wright, Roylance, with Mr. Ashton (clerk), Messrs. Diggle and Swinton (surveyors), and Mr. Farrington (inspector). The proceedings were private but we understand that it was expNained by the deputation that the company's only reason for closing the tidal openings was to prevent the admission of silt from the river into the waterway and thus reduce their dredging expendi- ture. It was stated that if the closing of the openings were allowed, the arrangement would not have the effect of raising the normal level of the water in the Canal, and this was assured by the apparatus they had for effectively dealing with the water. The proposed works would not therefore leave the district in any different position than as at present from a sanitary point of view, as they would not interfere with the sewer outfalls of the district, and render the water more impure. It was pointed out that the sewage water from above Latchford would not reach Runcorn, beyond what might escape through the locks, as the Company would divert it into the river by means of sluices at Woolston. Several ques- tions were put by members of the Rural* Council and answered by the officials, and it was suggested that the authority would require a clause in the Bill safeguarding their interests. Nothing definite, however, was resolved upon. At the Council Chamber, Town Hall, the Ship Canal deputation waited upon members of the Urban Council. Mr. P. Speakman, J.P., C.C., occupied the chair, and there were also present Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, Dr. McKenzie, Messrs. E. Imison, J.P., S. Taylor, J.P., C.C., J. W. Lightburn, James Pritchard, D. Bisbrown, John Wilson, J. Jones, W. Collier, T. Dean, G. Hewitt, H. J. Grice, R. H. Posnett, G. H. Kirkham, M. Sweetman, Dr. McDougall (medical officer). Mr. F. A. Lake (law clerk), E. Marshall (secretary), J. Wilding (surveyor). The de- putation gave similar explanations to those given to the Rural Council, and considered that the proposed works would tend to cleanse the water. A passing reference was made to the fact that the agreement between the Runcorn Council and the Ship Canal Company had now been practically carried out. The members of the Coun- cil have determined to meet shortly when they will fully consider the explanations made and decide upon what action to take.

===== 0002601_18960125_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD:

The first meeting of the new Board was held in the Board room, Town Hall. on Tuesday evening. mere were present Messrs. J. Pritchard, S. Taylor, J.P., C.C., E. Imison, J.P., F. J. Norman, J.P., and G. Moss (clerk).

ELECTION OF CNAIR3I&N. .The Clerk stated that the first business of the new board was to elect a chairman.—Mr. Taylor said he had very much pleasure in moving that Mr. Pritchard be the chairman for the ensuing three years. They had had Mr. Pritchard's experience as chairman for a number of years; and he was sure he had filled the position with credit to himself and credit to the Board. He was cer- tain 'they could not have a better chairman, and he had very great pleasure in moving that Mr. Pritchard occupy that position.—Mr. Norman seconded the proposition remarking that the chairman must have a thorough grip of the work, and it was a decided advantage to have a chairman who had that grip.—The proposition was carried.—Mr. Pritchard then took the chair,and thanked the members for the honour they had conferred upon him. In addition to the ordinary duties and work of the Board they would have other duties to fulfil during this coming period in deciding upon plans for new schools and the erection of the same on the vacant land in Victoria-road. It would also be necessary to make certain structural alterations in Granville-street to meet the requirements of the Education Department, and when that work was done there must be some re- arrangement of the standards and teaching staff which received Some attention by the last B3ard. He trusted he should have their hearty co-operation, and that the members would work harmoniously and agreeably to- gether.

THE NEW VICE-CHAIRMAN. Mr. Norman stated that he had it on the authority of Mr. Imison that Dr. McDougall could not accept the position of vice-chairman. Under those circumstances he had very great pleasure in proposing that Mr.lmison occupy the position. It was unnecessary to make a long speech on Mr. Imison's good qualities. Ho was ex- ceedingly well-known in the town, and he (Mr. Norman) hoped Mr. Imison would be able to accept the position. —Mr. Taylor seconded, and said he was sure none in Runcorn had taken a deeper interest in educational matters than Mr. Imison,and no one would occupy the position better.—The Chairman remarked that the mem- bers would be very glad to have Mr. Imison in that , position and hoped he would be induced to accept it.— The proposition was carried unanimously.—Mr. Imison said he wished their choice had been thrown on some one else. Personally he should be glad to have seen gemeone else occupying the position. He should be pleased to do all be could but at the same time he hoped he should not occupy the position for any length of time and that he might be allowed to retire and make room for another gentleman more suitable than he felt himself to be. He would take the earliest opportunity of doing

THANKS TO TSB PAST VICH-CHAIRMA.N. Mr. Norman proposed a resolution that the Board learned with regret that Dr. McDougall would not again accept the position of vice-chairman, and cordially thanked him for the services he had rendered to the Board in the past as vice-chairman. Mr. Norman re- marked that it was unfortunate the vote was omitted at the last meeting.—Mr. Taylor seconded the motion, stating that they had not a member of the Board that had taken a deeper interest in the work than Dr. Mc- Dougall. Although they had not at all times agreed, yet they had agreed to uiffer. As vice-chairman of the Board Dr. McDougall had acted to his own credit and to the satisfaction of the Board.—Mr. Imison supported the motion and said Dr. McDougall had taken an im- mense amount of interest in the work of the Board, and he, equally with Mr. Norman, regretted that he had not seen his way to again accept the position he had occu- pied with so much advantage to that authority and the public generally--Mr. Pritchard had great pleasure in adding his testimony to the punctuality and regularity of Dr. McDougall in his attendance and the deep interest he had taken in the education work of the Board. He had devoted much time to the work, at great inconveni- ence to himself on many occasions, and his services had been of considerable advantage to the Board.—The motion was carried unanimously.

THE COMMITTEES. It was also decided that the School Attendance Com- mittee and the School Management Committee consist of the whole members of the Board. This concluded the business.

FUNERAL OF MISS lIILL, OF RUNCORN.

There was a most impressive funeral at Runcorn on Monday afternoons when the mortal remains of Miss Mary (Polly) Hill, 27 years of age, daughter of Captain Hill, were interred in Runcorn Cemetery. The deceased had associated herself with Newtown Mission Sunday School, and for many years had fulfilled the duties of Sunday school teacher. She was also a district visitor, and in many ways had been a zealous worker in connec- tion with St. Michael's Church. She was of a most genial disposition, and had endeared herself to many. This was demonstrated on Monday when a large number of people manifested their respect to her Memory, and sympathyt with her relatives, by their presence. A pro- cession was formed at the house of the deceased's par- ents in Surrey-street. and was headed by teachers and I.ctvolars of the Sunday school connected with St. Michaers,who were succeeded by Dr. McKenzie,Captains Inglesfield, John Ellis, Heasley and Le Couteur ;Messrs. M. Thomas, John Jones, H. Horton, Peter Hadley, W. Bradshaw, J. Horsefield, and W. Carroll. The hearse followed, and on the polished oak c.ffin and also on the hearse were many floral tributes. The relatives and friends present were : —Masters James and John Hill (brothers), Captain H. Hill (father), Messrs. Frank Cringhall, George Crowther' William Charlton (Burnage), Harry Hill (cousin), James and Harold Houghton (cousins),Fred Sherratt (Widnes) Henry Charlton (Burnage), %Villain Cringhall, Dennis Cringhall, John Newton (Altrincham); and Herbert Hill (Heaton Mersey). The cortege proceeded to Michael's Church where the first part of the service was zonducted. The Rev. H. C. Moyle and the choristers met the pro- cession at the church gates. The service was fully choral and was most impressive throughout. The deceased's favourite hymn " When our beads are bowed with woe "was sung,and as the congregation departed, Mr. C. Timmins, jun., the organist, played the " Dead March," in Saul. The service at the grave side was brief, and at the close the hymn, " Jesu, lover of my soul " was sung. A large number of wreaths had been sent.

THE "GAY PARISIENNE" AT ST. •

An exceptional treat is being provided for the patrons ot the Theatre Royal, St. Helens, next week, when the eminently successful musical comedy of" The Gay Parisienne will be produced. This famous comedy has had a run of brilliant success throughout thecountry and it is generally anticipated that its visit to St. Helens will not prove an exception. The author is Mr. George Dance, who alo wrote the popular "Lady blavey and the piece is full of genuine hearty merriment from beginning to end. It will be produced by Messrs. Erskine , and Macdona's No. 1 Company, including Miss Nellie ' Hodson, who takes the title role, Mr. Albert James (" Ebenezer Honeycomb "), Mr. Charles Macdonna, as "Major Fossdvka," and Mr. Charles Seguin as "M. Poitipier." As will be seen from an advertisement in another column, the orchestra will be augmented for the occasion and will be under the direction of Mr. Knight Pearce.

CRUELTY TO A HORSE.—Alfred Bate, 59, olive-st., was summoned for working a horse while unfit on the 10th Jan.. Ernest Basking, 198, Longford-st.. was charged with causing it to be worked.—Inspector Bilston, R.S.P.C.A., said he saw Bate in charge of a horse attachsd to a coal lurry. It was lame in both fore-legs, aged, and in a low condition. He told witness that he could not help it and he had told Haskins about it. Witness said he had warned Haskins several times and he thought Bate was more the victim of circumstances, and that Haskins had compelled him to take the horse out.— Bate was ordered to pay costs and Haskins fined £1 includ- ing costs. WIFE DESERTION. AN OBSTINATE HITSBA.I4D,—Arthur Wright Whalley,66, Catherine-st.. was charged with desert- ing his wife since the 4th Jan.—Mr. Browne said that a fort- night ago he appeared for the c >mplainant at the court, and Mr. Fofshaw for the defendant and they cams to an agree- ment that he should contribute 6s. a week to her mainten- ance. This he had not done, and he had said he did not in- tend to maintain her. ALLEGED THEFT FROM THE PERSOL—Themas Walsh was charged in custody with having stolen from the person of William Norbury on the 27th July a silver lever watch and two steel watch-guards, valued at £3 10s.—The Chief Con- stable (Mr. L. Talbot) said that on the 27th July last William Norbury came into the town, and at about four o'clock in the afternoon entered the Higher Angel public-house in Butter- market-street. He had some beer, and after a short time had elapsed he left the place and went into Hill-street. Here he se-Aned to have come into contact with the prisoner. When he returned to the Higher Angel he missed his watch and guard, and subsequently reported the fact to the police. From inquiries it was found that within an hour after the articles were stolen they had been pawned with Mr. Joseph H. Houghton by the prisoner. They were handed over to Detective-Sergeant Monaghan, and a search was commen,ed for the prisoner. He was arrested on the previous afternoon by Monaghan, who met him in Scotland-road 'I he officer charged him with the theft. but Walsh replied that he had found it in Hill-street. Later on. however, he said he wished

to make a voluntary statement—with the apparent desire to exculpate s3rneone else. He said " I stole the watch myself. I pawned it myself, and I had no confederates." Conclud- ing. the Chief Constable asked for a week's remand so that further inquiries might be made.—Detective-Sergeant Monaghan deposed to apprehending the prisoner in Scotland- road on the previous afternooa at about 2 30; and otherwise supported the Chief Constable's s atement.—Prisoner was remanded for a week to await investigations. "THE TIMES" AND DR.

JENNER.

Dr. Jenner stated that the vaccination sore shculd, if it had taken properly, look on the eighth day like " a pearl on a red roseleal." The " Times," after discussing Dr. Jenner's discovery and upholding all his ideas, re- cently strongly urged that vaccination should be at once enforced, and the faddists who decry Dr. Jenner checked, by the authorities adopting a saong policy. Everyone should ne forced to, if possible, exhibit on the eighth day " a pearl on a red roseleaf." To secure this pretty effect a well-known Bachelor of Science recently stated that good pure vaccine lymph and clean instruments were required. He forgot to add that the wound also required to be attended and cleaned by a competent doctor, or if that was impossible, and it generally is, because the doctors always say, " It's only vaccination, and does not need attention," something should be done to kill the germs that en'er easily in thousands through the unprotected wound, which, of course, quickly becomes foul. He naturally omitted this, however,because he knew that,once the germs had entered the blood,all the old remedies and aotiseptics which were strong enough to kill the germs in the blood would also kill the child—or even man or woman—to whom they might be given. It seems a great pity that someone had not informed this worthy scientist that a new antiseptic remedy had recently been discovered, which, though it was absolutely harmless to the child, could be relied upon if;given to it to pass unchanged from its stomach to its blood, and there destroy and remove every one of these germs as fast as they entered the blood through the vaccination sore. He should also have been told that if the lymph was impure and this remedy used ,the action would be the same,r 'AAP disease germs, which are not so strong as the lymph itself, would be all killed as they reached the blood, and yet the effQcts of the lymph would be unchanged, thus pro- ducing the " pearl on the red roseleal." Lest anyone who reads this may not know of this long looked for remedy,we are pleased to tell them, first, that its action is unfailing, and that in the same way it will cure old cases of disease resulting from vaccination, though of years standing. and also that it is most appropriately named Vaccination Virus Vanquisher, or V.V.V., and that it can be obtained from all chemists at 2s. 93. per bottle or from Barclay and Sons,Limited, 95,Farrington- st., London, or from the Vaccination Virus Vanquisher Company, Limited, 31, Snow Hill, London, E.C., who also send free pamphlet to all who apply.

WARRINGTON.

P.S. A. AT THE T. .C. A. At the meeting on Sunday, the number of mem- bers present was the largest yet recorded. Mr. Kent (president) occupied the chair, and gave a hearty welcome to all the new members, and in the course of his address described the objects of the move- ment. The speaker for the day was Mr. Arthur Mounfield, who gave a most instructive and inter- esting address, taking as his subject" Some useful mad m en," OIL .—GARLAND, Importer, 51, Bridge-street, Warringten. CANTATA. AT ELLESMERE-STREET SCHOOLS.

A-C-antata. entitled " Ptineess Snow-white, or the Seven Dwarfs," was given in a very praise- worthy manner, at the Ellesmere-street schools on Monday evening, by the members of Bank-street Mission choir, assisted by the Sunday scholars. The various scenes were excellently depicted, and the characters entrusted to the scholars were pourtrayed with a great amount of credit. The singing of the children was especially good, in fact the whole of the performance bore ample evidence that they had been diligently trained by their teachers. The en- tertainment was fully appreciated by a large atten- dance of adults, many of whom were the parents of the children. The event had been organized to augment the Bank-street Mission fund. The per- formance was repeated on Tuesday evening with equal success. SEWING MACHINES Hand 23 10s• Treadle £4 43 with handiome walnut cover.—J. and W. DUTTON, 16 and 18, Sankey-at., Warrington.—Advt. W 00D.

At the monthly meeting of the Warrington Liter- ary and Philosophical Society, held on Monday evening in the Lecture Room of the Museum, the President (Mr. William Owen) said he intended at the next meeting of the society to ask them to pro- poge a resolution requesting the Town Council to pave with wood that part of Museum-st. in front of the building. Both the Museum and the School of Art now belonged to the Corporation,and it was ad- visable that the improvement should be effected.— Mr. L. Greening said he happened to speak to the Chairman of the Paving and Sewerage Committee the other day about the matter, and bad been told that the subject had been brought under the notice of the committee, and he understood it had been very favourably received. But still if a resolution as suggested by the President was passed, it would no doubt help forward the movement. (Hear, hear).—The President said he had no idea until Mr. Greening spoke that the matter had been under consideration. They would leave the matter over until the next meeting, and perhaps it would be found in the meantime that there would be no necessity ta forward a resolution from the society.

SEWING NIAMINF.9 Hand £2 188.64. Treadle £44s. with handsome walnut cover.—T. and W. Dtrrrox 16 and 18. Sankev-at.. Warrinoton. —Advt. A NEW CHAPEL FOR THE REV. EDWARD MOORE. The memorial stones of a new Baptist Chapel, of which the Rev. Edward Moore, formerly of War- rington, is to be the pastor, were laid on Monday afternoon, in Stanley-road, Bootle. For a number of years past the old Baptist Church in Derby-road has been struggling under great difficulties, arising I chiefly from the deterioration of the neighbour- hood in which the chapel was situated. About 12 months ago, however, a sale of the Derby-road building was effected, and a temporary iron struc- ture was during the past year built on the new site in Stanley-road, and in it the Baptists have for some months past been worshipping with aug- mented congregations and encouraging results generally. The new permanent edifice now in course of erection, at a cost of nearly .C 3,000 is situated in Stanley-road adjoining the proposed recreation ground of the Bootle Corporation. The total length of the church will be sixty-eight feet, and it will accommodate 300 persons on the ground floor and 120 in the gallery. The style of architecture through- out is Romanesque. Mr.W.FI all, of Liverpool,is the contractor, the architects being Messrs. Richard , Owen and Son. From the pnceeds of the sale of the old chapel, after paying for the iron building and defraying other preliminary expenses, there is in hand about £,BOO, and since the sale was effected a sum of £267 has besides been raised in cash and promises. At-, the memorial-stone laying on Monday , there was a large gathering, amongst those present being the Mayor and Mayoress of Bootle (Mr. and Mrs.l. A. Mack), Mr. J. H. Farmer (Town Clerk),Mr. R.Blease (president of the Liverpool Baptist Union), , the Revs. E. Moore (pastor), E E. Walter, C.Green, H. W. Hawkes, W. Bathgate, H. Bone, H. Cordon, and E. Morley.

NORTHERN RUGBY UNION.

WARRINGTON v. BRADFORD.

Saturday .was the date of the first visit of the famous Bradford team to Warrington, and a very large crowd of between 7,000 and 8,000 were present at the Wider-- pool enclosure to welcome them. The first meeting of the teams at Bradford ended in a most disastrous defeat of 23 points to nil,the heaviest defeat sustained by War- rington this season, but as the famous " strike " took place on that occasion, and as the team has been almost entirely reconstructed since then, the match offered no indication ot what would be the result on the return match. Warrington have since the advent of the new year been playing much better,and have been rather un- fortunate to lose several matches, and consequently it was felt locally that provided Warrington had a little slice of luck, the result of the first meeting would be shown to be quite wrong. The day on Saturday was a splendid day,, both for players and spectators, and this in addition to the" bill of fare" no doubt added tre- mendously to the " gate." The teams each underwant slight alterations. For Warrington, Burton was unable to play, and Barber accordingly went centre, and Carey took the vacant position at the wing. Bradford were minus Dobson, 'Foothill and the two Sugdens. The following composed the teams :

WARRINGTON : Heesom, full back; Carey, Barber. Cross and Lawless. threequarters; Foden and Clark,half backs ; Burrows, Nevins, Taylor, Barrett, Thornton, Andrews, Fairhurst, and Swift, forwards. BRADFORD: Smith, full back ; Cooper, Crompton, Bairstow and Jenkinann, threequarters ; Hill and Booth, half backs ; Firth, Edmuedson. Robertson, Robinson, Marshall, Wilson, J. Smith and Fearnley, forwards. Crompton kicked off in the face of a stiffish breeze, and no return being made, things looked ominous for Warrington when the first scrimmage took place in their own 25. The scrimmage was carried by Bradford, and Heesom just saved on the line. Foden, by a fine piece of play and a kick to the quarter flag, relieved the apprehensions of the Warrington spectators,and Nevins, Taylor and Barrett, by a good dribble gained still further ground, and another combined dribble almost ended in a score. Fine Passing between Hill, Crompton,Bairstow and Jenkinsnn,wae put an end to by Carey, and a reply was made by Clark, Foden, Cross, Barber and Lawless, who got close to the line, a lrng punt down the field by Cooper might have relieved Brad- ford considerably, hut off-side tackling by the Bradford forwards completely nullified the advantage, and a scrimmage was ordered near the line.Seieral scrimmages occurred in close vicinity to the goal, and from one. Taylor grounded the ball, but the referee refused to allow the point. Crompton and BAirstnw succeeded in working the play to the centre, hut Clark getting posses.- sion r in, closely followed by Foden to within a few yards of the Foam was too closely watched to get any advantage from the pass. S ,me hot scrimmages ensued in dangerous proximity to the Bradford goal line, and at length, Hill attempting to pass to Jenkinsen. Lawless dashed up and intercepted the pass and rushed over,thus opening the scoring for Warrington. Heesom took the kick, and only just miesed landing a goal. Warrington attacked with renewed vigour after the kick out, and it was surprising to see the forwards hold their own against the burly firadfordians. Clark was almost over within a minute afterwards, and Foden had a drop at goal which, however, failed and only a minor was gained. Smith just managing to kick dead before Barber was on to him. From the kick out, Bradford went away with a rush, and fine passing between Booth, Hill, Bairstow, Crompton and Cooper looked ominous for Warrington until the latter was brought to the ground by Heesom. • A. short attack was set up by Bradford,hut this was not dangerous and was soon repelled be War- rington, and soon fine passing by the flame halves and three-quarters took play again to the opposite end, and Smith on one occasion was almost beaten. Immediately afterwards, however, Barber made his mark almost in the midst of his opponents, and entrusting the place kick to Foden, that player kicked a fine goal and put his side seven points ahead. Simulated with their success Warrington attacked with astonishing vigour,and Carey had exceeding hard lines in failing to get over. Bradford gave a glimpse of what they could do immediately after- wards, and succeeded in getting to neutral quarters, where they were when the whistle blew for half time, the Tykes then being in a minority of seven points. Nevins kicked off after the interval and the first minute Carey was almost over. This was the result of a brilliant passing movement commenced by Clark, and taken part in by Foden, Barber, Cross and Carey. Carey dashed away along touch, and tricked both Crompton and Cooper, and he had got to the corner flag 'before he was tackled by Smith. Warrington wheeled the next scrimmage in. ,fine style. and Fairhurst carried Ithe ball over but the referee would not allow the point although there seemed to be very little wrong about it. Bradford now showed their sense of Warrington's superi- ority by having five threequarters, and shortly after- wards, Barber replied to this move, by taking Nevins out of the scrimmage and th.us equalling the number of the Bradford men. Chiefly by means of dribbles by Edmondson and Robertson,the half way line was reached, and Cooper dribbled nicely passed the Warrington men, and getting to Heesom, he kicked over his head and then rushed after the ball. Atry under the posts seemed to be certain, when Barber was seen tearing away at full speed after him. To the delight of the Warring- tertian% it was seen that he was the fleeter of the two, and just aa the line and the hall was reached, he passed Cooper, and kiclred the ball dead.amid the loud applause of the spectators. Warrington had slowed down now, and seemed content to play on the defensive,and Heesom showed his defensive powers well. Numerous minors fell to Bradford, but during the last five minutes, War- rington appeared to have got their second wind, and played up in a manner which boded ill for Bradford. Carey, Lawless, and Barber were the means of the bal. being taken to the opposite end, and a mast determined attack was made on the Bradford lines Warrington evidently meant business, and also intended Ito improve their score record. One of the Bradford players attempted to get the ball away but sent it in touch only a few yards from the line. and Foden seizing his opportunity, took the ball ha play, and before Brad- ford could get over their stupefaction at this unexpected movement, he had grounded behind the goal posts. Brad- ford protested strongly ag7aanst this point, but after con- sultation with the touch pdge who showed that Foden had not done anything not in accordance with the rules, the loud shnirta of the crowd.announced that the try was allowed. Foden was entrusted.with the place kick and was successful, thus scoring nine points himself during the afternoon. After the kick off, Warrington were again coming with a rush to the line, when the whistle blew for the cessation of hostilities and with Warrington winning a victory with the score of two goals (one from a mark), and one try (12 points) to Bradford nil. War- rington thus gained their greatest victory of the season against the team who had inflicted upon them their greatest defeat.

Sir D. Radcliffe, chairman at the Liverpool Tramways Company, informed the shareholders of that Company at the annual meeting on Saturday that the directors had had under consideration for some time the question of changing from horse to mechanical haulage. The Corporation would not allow them to use steam ; the compressed air system was found to be far more costly than horse haulage ; and as to electricity applied by means of the accumulator system, the estimate exceeded by 15 per cent the present system' in addition to which the cars would cost them about £368,000. The cable system had been found to be out of the question, so for the present they would continue to use horses. CARTER'S LITTLE 'ER PILLS.' ,4114 k
