THE ARMENIAN ATROCITIES.

MEETING AT WARRINGTON. On Tuesday evening a meeting was held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-st., Warrington, for the purpose ot hearing an address from the Rev. Prof. Thoumain, the well-known Armenian exile. Mr. _ _ . .

E. J. Guest., J.P., took the chair._ There were also

on the platform 'the Revs. John Yonge,J. S. Hughes, G. D. Mason, and W. H. Kirtland; Councillors H. H. Roberts, J.P., and Arthur Bennett, and Messrs. Robert Garnett, J.P., John Crosfield, J.P., Thomas Birchall, J.P., William bong, J.P., in addition to

Professor Thoumain. There was a large attendance. . _

_ At the commencement of the meeting, the Rev. Jour; YONGE read several letters of apology for non-attendance. We give extracts from two of the most interesting. Mr. Robert Pierpoint. M.P., for Warrington, wrote :—" I have to acknowledge the receipt of your invitation to attend a meeting at the Co-operative Hall,Sankey-street, on the 21st inst. It wid be out of my power to attend the meeting, as I leave England on the 17th for Cyprus. In view of the grave crisis in our own affairs through which we are now passing, I question the policy of hold- . .

ing a meeting concerning the unhappy state of affairs in Turkey. We all of us view with sorrow the lot of those who suffer from the misrule of the Porte, but see- ..

ing that we cannot by ourselves set things to rights in Turkey, and that at present we appear to be looked at with much unfriendliness by at least one of the great Powers, and probably by more, it would, in my opinion, be a grave mistake for England to offer to say or do anything which might mean the use of armed force in Turkey, and the plunging of Europe into a terrible war on the Eastern Question. The misery and slaughter which that would entail upon our own people and on others would far outweigh even the horrors which have occurred lately in Asiatic Turkey. In view of the above, whilst expressing my great sympathy with the sufferers, I cannot say that I am in full sympathy with the meet- ing." The Rev. W. H. Drummond wrote: "There seems to be a disposition in many quarters to postpone the day of reckoning, and to congratulate ourselves if we are able to delay by a year or two the re-opening of the Eastern Question. That delay is purchased at the cost of the _ .

e—xtermination of a Cliristian nation, and I have Yet to learn that we have any right to purchase our own peace at such a frightful cost The whole question of Turkish misrule, especially in its relation to Christian populations, is one that must be facea and settled. It is

time that Englishmen honestly gave up in the sight of the world their sentimental feeling for Turkey. . . In my opinion there is no shadow of doubt that Our friendship with Turkey in the past, and our special inter- ventions on her behalf, have helpsd to make these appal- ling massacres in Armenia possible. I confess that the reflection is anything but a pleasant one. But now to make any pretence of continuing that friendship, or by our silence to show that we are still prepared to treat Turkey as a civilised power, seems to me little short of an outrage upon all that is worthiest in our national character." (Applause.) The CHAIRMAN said he felt it a great honour to preside over such a meeting as that,and he was glad thatthere had been such a hearty response. He was sure they were all heartily sick and tired of seeing the fearful reports in the papers from time to time of the atrocities which were committed, greater than anything that history recorded since the time of Nero. They were disposed to do all they could to urge the Government to put a stop tothose things. Our Government was responsible next to Turkey for these fearful occurrences in America. We had held up the hand of the "sick man" and given him a power to strike as he had struck to-day. Personally he was disposed to favour the idea of this country dealing with the matter single-handed. It was a difficult matter indeed but if the other powers would not act we should be justified in acting single-handed against Turkey. (Hear, hear and applause.) He did not think Turkey would fight us but would give in, and we should then soon put an end to the terrible evils. There were some who said Arsoenia was as much to blame as Turkey but there could be no question that the way in which Turkey had acted was outrageous beyond expression. (Hear, hear, and applause.) There were some again who said that the reports were exaggerated but he did net believe that the correspondents of the English papers exaggerated such things. We had,he said, trustworthy reports. He would to God that the reports were only one-tenth true, but he feared we must accept them as all true. The in- terest of that meeting was centred in one who knew the Turk better than he did and he would not take up their time in telling them what perhaps they knew as well or better than he did. (Applause.) Prof. THOIIMAIN then gave a stirring relation of the conduct of the Turks in Armenia to the Christian popu- lation and produced facts as to their atrocities which called forth many expressions of resentment and indig- nation against the oppressors. These deeds were not only committed by the Kurds and other outlaws, but by the Sultan's soldiers. He (Professor Thoumain)himself was an exile and had been in captivity in Turkey and had worn some of the instruments of torture which he now exhibited to the audience. The Armenians, he said,were a peaceable and quiet Christian race, and the occurences which the officers of the Sultan were pleased to call re- bellion were nothing but the natural defence of life, pro- perty and religion by the downtrodden Armenians. Tbe Armenians were Christrians and would remain so ; they would give up their goods and even their lives but never their religion. (Loud applause.) Mr. W. LONG said he could assure them that he felt very deeply the honour and duty that was cast upon him by the promoters of the meeting, in asking him to propose a resolution. He was proud to stand upon that platform as he felt that England had at last risen, and could rise, and he hoped always would rise when her feelings were stirred above the considerations of party. (Applause.) Professor Thoumain was a man whose from heartwas upon his lips,and who had spoken to them the heart and with a remembrance of cruel massacres, still vividly before his imagination. From his own ex- perience he could confirm every word of Professor Thou- mainn'a as to the Turkish aim being to make out to the world at large that the Armenians were a trouble- some and turbulent people. He had spent some time in the East, and when returning by boat there was a party of several hundred Armenia's refuges on board. They were going to South America, and he had since thought how for- tunate they were. All present were agreed that some- thing should be done, and they should, therefore, press upon the Government the fact that they had the public of England behind them in anything they might do. (Applause.) He wished that all departments of the Government had behaved towards the treaty obligations in the aay that Mr. Chamberlain had behaved in observ- ing our treaty obligations in his conduct just lately in reference to the Transvaal. (Hear, hear, and applause.) He then moved the following resolution :—" That this meeting, comprising various sects and parties in the town and district of Warrington, regards with feelings of abhorrence and indignation,the atrocious events which have transpired in Armenia, under the Turkish rule,and strongly urges her Majesty's Government to do every- thing that can be promptly and judiciously done to pre- vent the recurrence of such calamities, and to rescue the oppressed people of Armenia from the present inhuman and merciless persecution." Mr. JOHN CROSFIELD, who was received with applause, said he felt so indignant that he hardly knew how to control his speech. (Hear, hear.) This was not a poli- tical question but a question for England—(applause)— but he would say this that if there had been a Liberal Government in office the Tory Press would have howled like wolves at them for not taking action. They, the Liberals, had kept quiet. They had said to the Govern- ment, "Do your duty. You must quell this thing." (Applause.) The Tory Press had praised the Liberal party, and bad said they had behaved very well. He was always suspicious when the Tory party praised the Liberal party. (Laughter.) Ha had lived long enough to have seen wicked wars done by England. done by the Liberal party, done by the Tory party. There was no man who hated war more than he did or who loved peace better, but there was something worse than war, ard that was oppression and cruelty. (Applause.) In his lifetime there had been only one righteous war and that was the war of the Northern against the Southern states in America. (Hear, hear.) That was a war of freedom against slavery. (Hear, hear and applause.) We had been guilty, he was ashamed to say, of many cruel, wicked and unjustifiable wars. We were guilty of the Crimean war for which we sacrificed some of the best blood of England and 100 millions of money and did not get the value of a cent. when we had finished. (Applause.) We were now told in this Atmenia.n question that the Government must not interfere be- cause there was Russia, and thee danger of war. But there was something worse than war. Were we going to be afraid because of Russia. when it was our duty to in- terfere and protect our fellow-Christians ? Were we to be told that as Englishmen ? No I It was our duty to interfere. (Loud applause.) If the greatest statesman of this century the G.O.M. had been in power he would never have submitted to these Armenians being tortured, outraged, and massacred for six months in this way without interfering. (Hear, hear.) It made his (the , speaker's) blood boil to think that we the greatest nation lon the earth should allow that wretched, miserable, wicked ,Sultan to have the newer to do such things with his wretched troops. (Applause.) How long were we going to stand this sort of thing ? (Applause.) If he had been Prime Minister of England he would have gone to the Sulton and told him that if he did not stop that sort of work he would blow him, his palace, and the whole of his dominions to pieces. (Loud applause.) If Mr. Gladstone had been in power he would have told him those words too. (Hear, hear.) The Sultan was a hypo- crite and worse than a hypocrite. There was no lan- gua.ge bad enough to be used about him, and the whole of the Turkish government should be swept from the face of the earth. (Applause.) It was a disgrace to the civilization of Europe. (Applause.) He wished John Bright were alive; he would have told the Government something about it ; he was the only man that dared get up in the House of Commons and protest against the Crimean war. (Hear, hear). They were told that we must not say anything or we should get to war. Well, let us go to war. (Loud applause.) When they heard from Professor Thoumain that nighs about his actual experiences they should be convinced that it was time we did something. We had gone to war for many a wicked cause. It was justifiable in the sight

CLOSING OF SALTPORT. THE SHIPBUILDING STRIKE.

The Executive Council of the Amalgamated Secieta Engineers have put an end to the strike in the sn building trade. After further considering the ters",o offered by the employers they have decided to doge ne strike, and to order the men back to work. The elti men voted in favour of the acceptance of the ter:j, offered by the employers, but the Belfast men reje;,‘ them by a large majority. A member of th. 3 ExecuP4 Council has been sent to Belfast to lay the decisioo" the Council before the men there.

WARRINGTON INFIRMARY AND DIS-

PENSARY ANNUAL BALL.

--- The annual ball, in aid of the funds of the above institution, was held on Wednesday week, in the Assembly-room at the Lion Hotel. There was a large number of guests present, there being about 200. The ball-room had been tastefully decorated by' Mr. Ralph Johnson, and the music for dancing, which was kept up from nine until after two o'clock on the fol- lowing morning, was supplied by Mr. Pearson's band. The committee of management consisted of the following gentlemen: the Mayor, Major Pick- mere, Messrs. John White, Henry Greenall, Edward Greenall, Harrison, Tinsley, C. U. Broadbent, J. A. EMMANITEL CHURCH, WARRINGTON.

:The annual congregational tea party in connection with Emmanuel Church (Reformed Church of England) was held in the schoolrooms over Arpley Station on Thursday week, and was in every respect a very pleasant and successful gathering. An excellent tea was provided, the tables being presided over by Mrs. Reed, Mrs. Norman, Mrs. Allen, Mrs. Gandy, Miss Evans, Miss Lees, Miss Harding,Miss Monkokc. After tea there was A social gathering presided over by the Vicar, (Rev. Edward T. Reed) when a lengthy and interesting pro- gramme was rendered. The choir acquitted themselves very creditably in an anthem, "Come, join our glad thanksgiving" ; the Tyrolese song of liberty " Merrily Ho"; a part song entitled " The .New Year," and " rhe singer's good night." Mr. Campion sang in good voice "The Diver," and "Out on the deep," and in response to an: encore "Anchored." Mrs. Norman sang Very sweetly "The Garden of Roses," and a vocal duet by Messrs. J. Lloyd and W.Dickens was well received. Miss Evans and Miss E. Lees played a piano duet and Miss Lees a violin sllo accompanied by Miss Taylor. Mr. Whittaker was most successful with his solos on the con- certina, his series of national airs, imitation of the bells and a farmyard being loudlY applauded,and in respond- ing to a very hearty encore he gave selections from the "Bohemian Girl." Mr. H. M. Ashton contributed a humorous reading, and a Lancashire sketch by Mr. H. Gandy helped to make a really good and enjoyable pro- ' gramme. Miss Eva Lees accompanied throughout. Dur- ing an interval in the programme the Vicar made a statement as to work in connection with the church dur- ing the past year. The church debt had been reduced from £lBO to £4B. The various branches of work such as the Sunday Schools, Senior and Junior Christian En- deavour Societies are in a satisfactory state, and gene- rally the work of the church is encouraging- Last-night the annual Sunday school treat in connec- tion with Emmanuel Church wbs held in the school- room over Arpley Station. The juniors had tea at five o'clock and the elder scholars later. Afterwards a story entitled" Little Minnie; or the Sunshine of a Sister's Love," was told by the Rev. E. T. Reed, and illustrated by the magic lantern, manipulated by Mr. Harding. Following this came the distribution of book prizes to the children for regular attendance and good conduct. This business concluded, a selection of humorous slides Was shown, much to the amusement of those present.

===== 0002601_18960125_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON LICENSED VICTUALLERS'

BALL.

On Thursday week the licensed victuallers of Warrington and District held a ball which it is hoped will be repeated each year. It is 26 years since the Association had a similar gatheiing and the success which attended the one in Parr Hall this week augurs well for the future. There was a surprising unanimity of opinion as to the excellence of everything in connection with the ball and the familiar phrase "everything passed off without a hitch "was never more justly applied to any gather- ing. The decorations by Messrs. Hesketh and Dutton, of Sankey-at., were the subject of general admiration. In addition to the adornment of the hall itself the ante- rooms were handsomely furnished, and provided pleas- ing resorts for some of the tired dancers. Music was supplied by Mr. Anderton's Bijou Orchestra which, with instruments of a different class to those usually used on such occasions, emitted harmony which called forth general commendation. The supper, to which too much praise cannot be given, was catered for by Mrs. Booth, an old-time caterer for the Town Hall banquets. Mr. Parkinson, photographer, introduced an appreciated novelty by being in attendance at an improvised but wen-furnished studio in the gallery, to photograph these who wished to have a souvenier of the hall. The new patent light was used and Mr. Parkinson's venture must have been successful, judging from the number of cus- tomers he had. Mr. John Sampson had charge of the bar. Dancing was commenced at 8.30, and continued with unabated vigour until 3.45 in the morning. There beine some 250 guests. Messrs. J. Sampson and T. Walsh made capable M. C's. An efficiest committee of which Mr. G. Grounds was Secretary, Councillor Shillcock, Chairman, anti Mr. P. H. Lake, Treasurer, were responsible for ar- rangements, which were satisfactorily carried out.

LITTLE SA.NKEY PARISH COUNCIL.

A meeting of the above authority was held at Sankey Bridges Wesleyan schoolroom, on Thursday week, the chair being occupied by Mr. R. B. .Matthews. There were also present, Messrs. J. Corradine, %V. Brough, Jackson, Greenweli, B. Grimshaw, Williamson, and J. Smith (deputy clerk.)

ABSENCE OF THE CLERK. Mr. Smith explained that Mr. Tunstall, the clerk, wa.s unable.to be present owing to an engagement in London.

PASSING OF ACCOUNTS. Accounts to the amount of £1 103. 7d. were passed for payment. The deputy clerk said that that was all the Liability of the Couticil it the present time.

THE LIGHTING OF LIVERPOOL-ROAD.

Mr. Jackson asked whether they had power to increase the number of lamps in the parish.—Mr.Smith said that they would have to apply to the District Council, and the charge would fall upon the parish.—Mr. Jackson then called attention to the north side of Liverpool-road, There were no lamps on that side of the road from the borough boundary up to Ridgway's works, and it was very dark at nights.—Mr. Matthews suggested that the matter be delayed until the township went to the borough, when the whole district would be properly lighted.—Mr. Jackson said that they might have to wait another year or two, and people could hardly find the houses they lived in, especially on foggy nights.—Mr. Greenwell said that there were several streets such as Evelyn-st., which were not lighted.—Mr. Matthews said that perhaps it would be as well to have lamps put up in Liverpeol-ro ad, and then they would be up ready for next winter. He could not, however, recommend that the streets off Liverpool-road should be dealt with yet,as if they did not fully light them, when they went into the borough, the Corporation might say that they had been lighted according to the wish of the inhabitants, and T. robably would not put up any more lamps.—lt was then moved by Mr. Jackson, seconded by Mr. Cor- radine that the lighting authority of the parish be re- quested to place lamps between the borough boundary and Ridgway's works, and that a committee composed of Messrs. Greenwell, Jackson and Corradine be chosen to arrazge with the authority with regard to the plac- ing of the lamps. HALTON'S BRIDGE IMPROVEMENT. The Chairman said that he was the waywarden of the parish and at the meeting of the Highway Board held the previous day, there was a very important matter brought forward with reference to the proposed straightening of the road at Halton's bridge in Penketh. The County Council had agreed to pay for the whole of the straightening of that road by annual instalments for 1.5 years. but the Council would not bind their succes- sors. Unless there was a distinct promise' no banker would enter into any transactions, and if the Council would only give a definite promise, there would be no difficulty in the matter. This road was a very danget- ous one, and was much used by the inhabitants of Little Sankey who should endeavour if possible to get all the roads round about in proper order. No doubt the War- rington Corporation and the Widnes Corporation would also be approached in the matter. The subject did not effect the rates of Little Sankey in the least, but he wished them to join the other authorities in putting pressure on the County Council. He would ask them to pass a resolution, that the County Council use every means in their power to improve the road. That reso- lution would he thought strengthen the hands of the Highway Board in the matter.—Mr.Greenwell said that he was surprised that there was any lingering in the matter at all, as the road was very dangerous. He was afraid that some accident would occur if something was not dttne.—Mr. Brough then moved, and Mr. Greenwell seconded the resolution also that copies be sent to the Clerk to the County Council, the Chairman of the Hichways Committee, and Mr. Sutton, the representative of the district on the County Council. ALTERATIONS OF FOOTPATHS IN AIIPLEY MEADOWS. Mr. Matthews said that notices had been served by the London and North Western railway that they intended to alter various footpaths in Arpley Mead awe. He did not know whether the alterations would be for better or for worse for Little Sankey. Mr. smith informed him that he thought they would be better. Whether that was so or not it would be well to form a small committee to look after the business.—lt was decided that the com- mittee consist of Messrs. Matthews, Greenwell, Brough, and Grimshaw.

===== 0002601_18960125_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
VOTE OF CONDOLENCE.

Mr. Matthew a moved a vote of condolence with the family of Mr. Selby, the agent to Lord Lilford, who had recently died. He said that Mr. Selby had been intim- ately connected with the township as Lord Lilford owned all the land in that district.—Mr. Williamson seconded the motion which was carried unanimously.

PARENTS SUMMONED FOR CHILD

NEGLECT.

Before the magistrates at the Warrington Borough Court, on Friday, Ellen Evans and Thomas Evans, 8 Jolley's-yard, Friar's Green, were charged with wilfully neglecting their children, Gertrude, George, and Thomas, on the 27th December and during the previous six months.—lnspector M'Ateer said the defendants were the parents of three chil- dren, George 12, Gertrude 3,and 'Thomas 18 month. In consequence of a complaint be received be visited their former residence on the 27th December in company with Sergeant Smith and found the three children mentioned in a filthy condition. They were insufficiently clothed and very miserable look- ing. In the house was a Mrs. Smith, sister-in-law of Mr. Evans,and her three children. The two defend- ants were out. In the kitchen there was nothing but a broken box which was used as a table,and the room was filthy. In the front bedroom occupied by the two de- fendants and their children there was no furniture and only an old tick on the floor with no bed covering. In the back-kitchen which was occupied by Mrs. Smith and her two boys of 15 and 14 and her girl of 8, there was nothing but straw and that was filthy. Witness called again on the 30th with Inspector Spinks, and they found the house in just the same condition as before and Mrs. Evans was also there. He told her he would have to report the case, and she said her husband was only earning about £1 a week, and this was true. He had 7s. a week pension from the army and he had money enough to keep him and the children. Last Saturday morning witness visited the house agh in and found the new house in Friar's Green in the same condition as the other and the box was the only furni- ture. The Inspector submitted that this was a very bad case, and he thought a substantial fine ought to be inflicted or defendants be committed to gaol. Sergeant Smith supported this statement and added that the children were fairly well nourished and their bodies clean. Inspector Spinks also gave corroborative evidence. Dr. Bowden deposed to visiting the house on the 30th December and finding the children dirty and their cloths dirty. The other particulars given by the Inspector wev correct. The children were fairly well nourished.—Ser- geant Rowbotham also corroborated. Alderman Harrison to Inspector M'Ateer: Is it a ques- tion of drink or not? Inspector M'Ateer : I cannot say, sir; he has not 'men in Warrington more than three or four months. Chief Constable Talbot said defendant had got his dis- arge from the army as sergeant and he had his good conduct badge. The male defendant said he had his sister-in-law and her three children to keep in addition to his own on £1 a week. It was as much as he could do to support them. When the Inspector was there on the 30th the bedding was in the yard—a good flock bed. Mrs. Egal49, who was sworn to give evidence, said she gave the children as much nourishment as she could. It was quite true about the bedding. The magistrates took a lenient of the matter and ad- journed the case for a month,in order to give the accused time to make improvements.

SUICIDAL WASPS.

M. Henry, a Frenchman, being curious to seethe effect of benzine on a wasp, put some of it under a glatiin which a wasp was imprisoned. The wasp immediately showed signs of great annoyance and anger, darting at a piece of paper which had been introduced with the benzine into his cell. By and by, he seems to have given up the unequal contest in despair, for he lay down on his back, and bending up his abdomen, planted his sting thrice into his body, and then died. M. Henry allowed his scientific interest to overcome his humanity so far as to repeat the oxperinvnt with three wasps, only to find that the other two did likewise. He is, therefore, of opinion that wasps, under desperate circumstances,corn- a, mit suicide.

A CORRECTION. Sir,—l notice that by a slip of the pen I wrote " Ingolsby "in my communication of last week instead of " Ingersoll" ; the healthier impressions of the for- mer lingering, it would seem, more durably than those of the Anaerican windbag.—Yonrs obediently, ZAMINER.

CO-OPERATION IN PENKETIL Sir,—Last evening we were treated to a very interest- ing paper on Charles Kingsley in connection with the Mutual. The lecturer was Mr. Johnson, of Macclesfield, who is a Socialist, and who gave us an account of the co-operative movement in his town. The silk trade had so declined that the wale operator's average earnings for 10 years had been from 14s. to 153. per week, but had wider co-operative management increased to 2.5 s T j,see of course, is very satisfactory. Our regret . there is such a strong contingent of socialists, ir zet keth, that co-operation is not more successful tn- is a great pity that the first .attempt at - OM It e "the launching of the Penketn Park Co oheuld have 4er-operation .T.,roved so disappointing. Nothing sr So we can hardly be surprised thp' dslike success. So attempt. should be loolcf_dupon as doubt', cd. We are told the rea- son why cooperation is not - Vourishing is that there ,are too mauy dividend b'..inteali.lNow I think it is hardly en who have saved their reasonable to expect w,0,ki„,,,r4,,,m tlO or £2O, to Ihd &Ear an indefinite number of years without a Penny interest. No doubt others who have insad:a their vile in other companies (not open to workmen.) than - iperative, can with patience wait for a divi..iend and! . indifferently on even if non obe torthcrAning. I think we must all admit that co-operation carrif.rd on on the lines of the Macclesfield silk weavers, after all legitimate expenses are paid, the profits to be shared amongst the workmen in the proportion of their wages, is the ideal system for the working classes. The workmen also have their share in the management, which is another good feature. I would like to suggest that a Socialist of Mr. Johnson's or Mr. Tom Hughes' stamp be invited over to Penketh to look into the above schemes and point out the cause or causes of failure and submit the same with their advice to a public meeting. —Yours truly,

SOCIAL REFORMER.

MR. J. PEERS AND lIIS CRITICS.

Sir,—lt would be amusing if the subject was not a serious one, to read the letters of Mr. Peers and his cri- tics. I have no intention of attempting to defend Mr. Peers, who is quite equal to the task himself. but I con- fess to a feeling of disappointment on reading the letters of his three opponents in your last issue. I presume they are meant for replies to his statement. but I had to wade through half of each letter before finding any at- tempt at meeting his charges, and the attempt does not look like a great success. It rather seems as if the show of smart writing was meant to cover the deficiency in strong argument. Yet Mr. Peers is by no means invul- nerable, and some of his propositions may be described as only balV,truths which come, very near being no truths at alt.- His charges are very much as follows : (1). That professed Christian men, who in all the practical affairs of life are keerria detect fallacies, are, in matters supposed to be of eternal interest, willing to accept dogmas created in ages of darkness and ignor- ance, and which have no reasonable claim to acceptance. Well, Mr. Peers, I can only say with sadness that the truth of your charge is self-evident to any really thought- ful man. (2). That Jesus of Nazareth was not known as Jesus Christ by his contemporaries and "probably never heard the name as applied to himself." Well, again I am ready to admit that you may be verbally correct. But was it worth while specially emphasizing the fact? But do not forget another more important fact still, that the truths which that same Nazarene taught and lived have been felt through 1,800 years to be so applicable for the heal- ing of the world's sins and sorrows that he has been re- cognised by millions as the:truly "anointed" above his fellows. (3). That Calvinism is the logical and biblical form of sectarian Christianity. Well, if he will allow me to alter the phrase to " Pauline " Christianity, I will admit there is acme truth in the charge. It is now well-known that out of over a thousand sermons preached by Calvin in Geneva, not one text in a hundred was taken from the Gospels. And in the Westminster confession of faith, in the article "Justification by Faith," forty-two out of the forty-three proof texts are from the epistles and other parts of the Bible and only one from the Gospels. It is indeed amazing how much of the stereotyped orthodox theology is taken from Paul who never saw the Master, and from the other Apostles, who during his lifetime frequently misunderstood him and at the last forsook him and fled. (4). That the precepts of the New Testament do not teach men to abolish slavery. Again I admit the truth of the impeachment. For the Christian conscience in the days of the Apostolic writers was not so enlightened on this subject as is the Christian conscience of today. Even Pant himself sent back the slave Onesimus to his master without a word of reproach for his slave-holding, and within the last 50 years American divines have written strongly in defence of slavery as a divine institution. To them as to the dis- ciples of his own day the reproach of the Master is ap- plicable, "Ye know not what manner of spirit ye are of." But admitting all this,is it not the spirit of Christ's teaching that has given the death-blow to negro slavery and is destined eventually to annihilate all slavery in whatever shape it may exist? His great doctrine of the Fatherhood of God is only cow dawning in all its full- ness on the noblest spiritsof our race. Not the spurious Fatherhood of the Creeds which would consign millions of His children to eternal anguish for not believing dogmas about Him which make Him appear to be worse and less merciful than a bad man. All such conceptions are totally irreconcilable with the Fatherhood of which Jesus has given us glimpses in the parable of the Prodi- gal Son. hen will men take to heart the truth that inspiration and increasing knowledge of the works and ways of the divine government of the world are still as open to the earnest seeker of to-day as ever in the past,that bondage to the letter killeth— " That all the good the past has had Remains to make the future glad; That eAch true heart may be a shrine. And ev'ry land a Palestine." —Apologising for thus trespassing on your space, yours truly,

A LEARNER.

COUNCILLOR BENNETT ON THE lINENIPLOYED

AND IRELAND,

Sir,—ln reply to Mr. Bennett's letter of the 4th inst., let me say. 1. Mr. Bennett urges that he has" answered me twice already." Answer : What does he mean by saying he has answered ma? My charge against Mr. Bennett is that he used the following words—Speaking of the unemployed—(and not as he states in his letter of the 4th, of the unemployed problem) he said "they are only " a nuisance here." And in his S' reply" at the Debating Society's meeting he repeated the objectionable language and coupled with the unemployed the inmates of the workhouses. 2.—Mr. Bennett acknowledges the fact that he did use the words attributed to him. But, he says " never used the word of which Mr. Dalton complains in any such sense as he attaches to it." Answer : I would be the last person in the world to think of charging Arthur Bennett, Town Councillor, chartered accountant, Editor, Author, Poet," Sunrise Statesman." and Dreamer, with having used the objectionable word,in its most offensive sense ; which would imply that the poor people were so much filth or offal. What Ido maintain, is, that the word nuisance,aPPliedto God's poor is in its literal sense cruel, indecent, offensive, and vulgar. i 3. Mr. Bennett urges that t was an improper thing for me to discuss (he uses the word "drag "—being a poet)—the subject in the columns of your paper. Answer: If it was wrong for me to discuss the subject in your paper,it must be wrong for him to discuss the subject in your paper What is wrong in the Irish Home Rule goose, must, of necessity,be wrong in the new—Conser- vative—Cum-grano—Democratic—Ga nder ! 4. Mr. Bennett in tbe course of a few cheap sympa- thetic flights,and unmeaning platitudes,seeks to impress your readers with the virtues of new Conservatism. Answer :If the condition of the working classes is to be made better, it can only be done by sending men to Town Councils and to Parliamant who will try to alter the old state for the new. Your old fossil Tory will alter nothing that will touch his pocket; then the nest thing to that is your new Conservative—(God save us from all evil). But your new Conservative will vote for no one but a Tory at election times. So Mr. Bennett's platitudes are practically useless. Sympathy is cheap! What the people want is justice I and not mere sympathy ! But I pray that God will send the poor a better .advocate than a new Conservative, who whilst he dresses his attenuated figure in Democratic clothing, supports the " fossils" through thick and thin. " Faith without works is—dead." (There is nothing personal intended here. It is merely" poet's license" and is intended for the system and not for the unfortunate persons affected by it.) 5. Mr, Bennett charges me with being guilty of misrepresentation. Answer: Now, Mr. Bennett will i please observe, this s a serious charge to bring against a person. For it implies that I have falsely represented him. I first of all challenge Mr. Bennett to give your readers some evidence to support the charge be makes so lightly without one shred of proof? What is my defence? 1. Your readers will remember that I represented Mr. Bennett as having used the following words in speaking of the unemployed and the inmates of the workhouse. I

XN'OTJTING WITH THE SALVATION HA.RRIERS. " NoT,asearabtase plaintiff's atterney."—Legal Adage. Sir,—Kindly allow me to thank my numerous " critics" of last issue. Ido this not so much for what they say of nse but for what they so magnanimously re- frain from saying. The vocab-alary of polemical Billings- gate might have furnished still more material for per- sonal abuse, while the use thereof is so much easier than striving for reasoned conviction. Judging from that column and 1-7th of vehement vituperation,Tom Paine's "Age of Reason" has not yet arrived. There the sal- vation pack is in full cry, though some seem to have lost the scent. So I think .we had best "hark back" to the missionary tub and see if it be as bottomless as I think it. My contention now and heretofore is, that whatever good we may think there is in Christianity, we have not the right to force it upon people unwilling to receive it. And this is the case in India,and notably in China ,where, if these intrusive fanatics were not backed by British bayonets, they would be requested to leave at once. Eaucated natives both of China and India will tell you that this missionary mania is a failure as to its object, but too successful as a manufactory of low-caste hypo- crites, and places a premium upon dissimulation, the " converts " assuming and discarding their clothes and their Christianity with equal facility. This being so, ' why should we persist in spending men, money and ' might in order to perpetuate this monument of Pharisai- cal arrogance ? If John Smith, a zealous Protestant, went into the house of, say, Peter Robinson, a devout Catholic, and favoured him with a dissertation on Papal enormities with incidental mention of the Scarlet Lady, we should think John Smith got what he deserved when kicked out by Peter Robinson. In like manner John Chinaman would be quite justified in thus expediting the exit of John Bull. And, again, do we ourselves quite believe all the musty dogmatism we attempt to force down the yellows of China and the browns of India ? Christianity I is loudly advertised as a panacea for the cure of all ' imaginary diseases, good for exportation as for home consumption. Like other patent panaceas it is exported with the Government stamp. The difference between the medical and the theolegical quackery is:that the first pays for its stamp which the latter does not. Hence say that the missionary tub ought to stand on its own bottom,if it has one. At present it depends very much upon the purse strings of the British tax payer. More than one of my wrathfal critics—and it is very curious to note how easily stirred" believing " bile is— have advised me to study the " evidences " for Christi- anity and its origin. Excellent advise. Let us act upon it at once. I like going to the root of the matter ; it is the best way to eradicate error. The essential and central dogma of Christianiq is redemption by an atonement for original sin. Deprived of this element, Christianity would be eviscerated—the play without Hamlet. Original sin then is the foundation upon which rests the central dogma of Christianity. The fable upon ' which this rests again is a purely Pagan fable current long before a sentence of its Biblical transcript was written. The authorities of the British Museum will show any visitor ample evidence of the truth of this. So the essential element of the creed of Christendom has, for its origin and foundation, a Pagan fable. Now if Genesis, and its copy of the heathen fable be " unhis- tonic "(vide Prof.Bonney at Church Congress) what need is there for an atonement fo.• an " unhistoric " sin ? Now is there any wonder that educated men, Chinamen or Indian,should object to have theological offal like this dumped at their doors ? The fable itself has long been rejected by reading and reasoning people at home, and it only' requires the application of a little honesty and logical consistency to make the inevitable sequence of the rejection of the dogma and the creed embodying it.Origins,quotha Truly you shall have origins to satiation. Here is one. The origin of the atonement idea. This arose from the savage, utterly barbarous, and immoral conception of a savage and sanguinary God who required his revengeful wrath appeasing by the sacrifice of some innocent thing. Hence the Christian dogma of the atonement—the sacri- fice of the innocent to procure the safety of the guilty— a wholly savage creed worthy of the savages with whom it originated. Thus we see that Christianity has its roots deep in the Paganism from which by process of evolu- tion it has sprung. And abundant signs of this evolution are `still on it, if only men would look. But the aver- age Christian has long since "made up his mind "on this question, in a small-parcel labelled "Jesus only ; this side up with care.. Fragile—explosive." And woe to the man who attempts to turn it over to see if it has another side. And to the name, my rendering of which has so upset the mind of "Layman "—I might have still further startled him by a literal transcript from my Greek Testament—which I Presume would be Greet to him as it is to me and most of the readers of the " Examiner." This naturally suggests the i thought What was a Jewish capenter doing with a Greek name— Jesus Christ. He never had it. The name was one of the consequences of the Greek vehicle through which the tradition came down to us. Jesus Christ is never men- tioned either actually or incidentally in the Hebrew Scriptures. Finally.allow me to refer to your correspon- dent" Zaminer." It is amusing to see this little body— to whom " discretion is the better part ef valours"— scolding away for all he is worth. "It is the common style of this profuse scribbler when he has nothing in the shape of argument to offer to revile those whose reasonings he is unable to meet" !!! After which he reheves his "Christian" feelings by the repetition of such charitable bits as—"effrontery," "reckless assumptions," "blind prejudices," "crass ignorance," and other choice bits of Billingsgate. But this perhaps is the Christian way of" reviling those whose reasonings he cannot re- fute ?" What notion of Christian morality he can have who makes personal attacks under the cowardly covert of a pen-name I do not know, but I do think that opin- ions so expressed are not worth much with such endorse- ment. Judging from the bilious bitterness of his wrathful outpourings I should say that he has suffered somewhat under another name in your columns. How- ever, I have no ill-feeling for this little body or any other, but wish you, and them, a prosperous New Year. —Yours truly,

JOHN PEERS.

THE AGED PEr;SONS TREAT AT

RIJ NCORN.

In the Council Chamber at the Town Hall, Runcorn- on Monday evening', the annual meeting of the commit tee of the Aged Persons and Plot' Children's treat was held. The chair was occupied by Mr. E. Imison, J.P., and amongst those present were Messrs. S. Taylor, J.P., C.C., W. Crosby, T. Wood, A. iley, J. D. Jones, W. Millington, J. E. Meredith, and G. Howard. The balance sheet for the past year showed receipts amounting to £55 12s. 61, and the expenses to £49 2s. 9d., leaving in hand a balance of £6 9s. 91 This was adopted on the proposition of Mr. Taylor. A vote of thanks was ten- dered to the officers for their past services, and the secretaries were instructed to write to MiBA Pierpoint conveying their best thanke to the Tableaux Vivants Committee for their donation of £3. It was resolved that the treat be again given, tke gathering to be held in the Public Hall, on February 18th, and that the menu should consist of bread and butter, beef and ham. seen and currant cake, tobacco and tea. The committee then proceeded to the appointment of officers. Messrs. T. Cooke and Walter Millington were re-elected, and Mr. George Howard was appointed in the place of Mr. Jones, resigned, as hon. secretaries. Mr. J. B. Holland was re-appointed treasurer; and Messrs. Meredith and Linaker were re-chosen as auditors. The com- mittees were appointed as follows : Collecting, Messrs. lmison, Taylor, F. J. Norman, Frith, Stafford, ISmith, Coventry, Wood. Woodland, Reynolds, Wes- ton, Bellamy, Posnett, Poole, Wiswall, Lake, W. Crosby, and Meredith; tea and provisions :—Messrs, Smith, Bowyer, Coventry, Shaw, Crosby, Ward, Collier- Robinson,and Reynolds;hall committee :—Messrs. Wood, land. Poole, A. Riley, Williams, Shaw, Byfield, T.Leach. and G, A. Hayes ; entertainment committee :—Messrs, James Littler, J. Holforti, J. Leathwood, C.Le Contour. W. Cooke, and W. Crosby; stores committee :—Messrs. Sweetman and H.Hughes ; cutting committee :—M.ssrs. Sutton, Bowyer, Howard, Williams, P. Leathwood, T. Riley, Taylor, Langshaw, Sweetman, and Moulton ; cabs :—Messrs. Williams, 14,ttler, A. Riley, Parry, J. Ellison, and D. Littlemore.--The meeting then termi- nated.

THE EXAMll‘ftil, SATURDAY, etANITARY 25, 16

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

lhe great surprise in tha matches in the Northern League on Saturday,was the crushing defeat of Bradford by Warrington. The victory of Warrington was no fluke either, for Bradford were fairly and squarely beaten on their merits. It was quite a treat to the Warrington section of the spectators to see the way in which the light Warringtonians put the burly Bradfordians through their paces. After seeing the match on Saturday, it is hard to believe that the team which was defeated was the very team with only about three alterations, which ' has inflicted the heaviest reverse upon Wardngton dur- ing the present season. Of course the Warrington team I had been completely metamorphosised, and this has no I doubt a lot to do with it.

That the bettor team won, no one who saw the match will for even a moment question. The Bradford men were almost large enough to eat Warrington, but the latter more than completely neutralised the advantage of weight by their superior agility and combination. Despite their weight, Bradford carried very few scrimmages,Warrington working with a will and heeling out and wheeling the pack at least five times out of every six. IL would be invidious to mention any names among the forwards as they one and all put in all they knew, and thus gave every opportunity to the backs.

It was at half back where Warrington claimed the greatest superiority. For a long time past, the critics of Warrington have been bewailing the want of a good half back, but since the new rule has come into operation, there has not been quite as much said, and after Satur- day, provided the Warrington halves keep up the same form, there need be nothing at all said. The way that Foden came out was simply marvellous. The new rules evidently suit him. As long as he plays as on Saturday his place on the team is secure. It was to him in a great measure that the victory was due. To score nine out of twelve mints in one match is a really great feat. He also showed that he was a goal kicker of no mean order. Clark was not quite so prorainent but he played a good game nevertheless, and made some fine runs.

To Lawless belongs the credit of opening the score for Warrington, and the try he scored was really the best of the two. Barber, Cross, Carer, and Hersom also put in some grand work, and sclrcel_y a mistake was made by any of them. For Bradtord,Smith played a very moder- ate game. Cooper was the hes:, of the Bradford three quarters. though his fame P. 3 a sprinter was somewhat tarnished by the way in which Barber overhauled him in a race for the line. He was caught up in a similar manner at Bradford in September by Broady, so that Warrington possess men quite as fleet or fleeter than Cooper. Crompton, once so popular in Runcorn did not do much, nor did Jenkinson, but Bairstow played well. Of the other men. Hill was the best half, and he show d that if he got half a chance he was a dangerous man indeed. Edmondson and Robertson were the best for- wards.

The victory was a feather in Warrington's cap, and though Bradford may not be quite as gold as last year, there is no team which the other members of the North- -ern League would rather beat. They are without doubt the most popular team in the League, and their success against Lancashire teams has been most marked. They have played 25 matches and only lost eight. Thirteen of these matches have been with Lancashire teams, and they have only lost three of these. Warrington shares with Widnes and Leif..o.l the honour of being the only Lancashire teams to beat Bradford this season.

This victory should be an incentive to Warrington at their match at Hull to-day. They have a defeat to re- venue here. Oldham is the only team vet to beat War- rington twice, and therefore Warrington should endea- vour to prevent any other team doine• so. Hull are pretty strong forward,and if they can he held there,Warrineon have a capital chance of reversing the verdict of the first match,which it must be remembered was at the expense of the team which went on strike.

The committee have chnsel the same team to go to Hull to-day that played on Saturday,with the exception that Clark, who was unfortunately hurt during tho last five minutes of the match, has been replaced by Higgin- botham, an old St. Mary's player. The following is the team : Heesom, full back ; Lawless. Barber, Cross, and Carey, threequarters ; Foden and Higginbotham, half backs ; Burrows, Nevins, Swift, Taylor, Barrett, Thorn- ton, Andrews, and Fairhurst. forwards.

St. Helens came a cropper on Stturdayand Stockport have now beaten them twice this season, and have the satisfactory record of 19 points to three against them. Their luck which was so prominent at the beginning of the season seems to be leaving them, for they lest the Halifax match by the penalty goal. In the beginning of the season, it was quite usual for them to win by a try or a penalty goal.

Widnes could not aet the better of Wakefield Trinity, nor could Wakefield Trinity get the betttr of them, and the result was a pointless draw. This was sufficient to prevent Wanington getting in front of them, though as the wire pullers have a game in hand,they have still the best record.

Leeds will have good cause to remember their matches with Runcorn this season, for their lines have been crossed ten times by the Cheshire club, in the two matches played. Runcorn have evidently got over the drubbing thay received from o!dhlm, and have still the satisfactory position of fifth in the table. Manningham have the championship both of the York3hire section,and of the whole League apparently safe, but still there is many a slip between the cup and the lip, and it would not take much to let either Halifax or Oldham slip in.

POST OFFICE SAVINGS BANK.

IMPORTANT NOTICE.

The Press Association understands that notices have been issued by the Postmaster-General calling public at- tention to the liPw life insurance tables which will come into force on February Ist, by which the annual pre- miums in respect of Post Office Savings Bank life insur- ance will be reduced, and the principle of old age insur- ance will be extended. Per..ions will be able to effect such insurances through the Post Office Savings Bank to mature at ages of 55, 60,and 65 years (or sooner in the event of death), instead of at 60 years only, to which such insurances have hitherto been limited. Beside these advantages there remain all the existing opportus nities of saving money and for providing against the uncertain future. Old age pensions, in the form of immediate or deferred annuities, may he secured. Ordinary deposits up to £5O a year and £2OO in all, and investments in Government stock up to £2OO a year and £5OO in all,may be made ; and even children and small investors are not overlooked. They may save even a penny at a time by means of the shilling, stamp deposit forms.

ODDFELLOWS' SUPPER AT RUNCORN

THE QUESTION OF OLD AGE PENSIONS.

The members of the Loyal Wright's Pride Past Grands' Lodge had their annual supper on Friday evening, at the Devonshire Hotel, Runcorn. Among those present were :—P. G. JoFeph Pritchard (chairman), P.P.G.M. George Burrows (vice-chairman;, P. G. Frsnk Cringhall (president of the lodge),P.G. K.J.Pendlebury (vice presi- dent), P,P.U.M.'s Robert Vizight (treasurer),H. B. Toone (secretary), R. Roberts, John Hilton, Peter Kilcross, W. Cringball, George Riley, William Powell, and George Rutter; P.D.G.M. W. E. Dudley, P.G.'s Rowland Jones, Joho Groome, William Ford, Greenway, Millington, George Millington, G. H. Walton, and William Wright. After a very substantial supper, well served by Brother Raining. The Chairman proposed the usual loyal and patriotic toasts,which were duly honoured,and P.I.G.M. Roberts responded for the Army, Navy, and Reserve ' forces.—Mr. Burrows submitted the Manchester Unity in a very brief address. The toast was heartily drunk.— The Chairman in resnonding said it was the spirit of in- dependence which actuated the founders of the great system of frienOly societies, which had done so much to foster brotherhood among them, and had taught many of the working classes what could be done by unity. It was the same spirit of independence which had estab- lished among them the great principles of trade union and co-operative eecieties, which, if rightly guided ,were certain to proch 'narked change iii the relationship of capital and la_ between masters and men. It was the same indepe --ece which now induced them to ask for a system of . aided old age pensions, so that the one great dread early all honest hard-workingmen, namely, that of ending their days in the Workhouse, or • what was sometimes worse,of dying of starvation, which might be removed. But there were many clear hard- headed men in the order who said that the pensions would only be another name for State-aided pauperism, and that they as Oddlellows would thereby lose their in- dependence. That, however, was not his opinion, for he believed the thing out to be done outside all efforts which might be put forward bo their existing friendly societies, for their funds were not yet of sufficient mag- nitude to grapple with the difficulty ; but yet from ex- perience returns ',light be made out which would enable the Government to see what would be required, and thus they might be induced to allocate a portion of the immense wealth of the nation to relieve the wants of the hard-working labouring poor, after they became in- capacitated:through the infirmities of old age.(Applause.) l'o those of their brethren who said that by being State- aided they would lose their independence he would put the question, Did the great ones of the laud do so by re- ceiving a State pension ? He instanced the Duke of Marlborough. Aeain, did the great army of officers and servants, in their militaryeaaval, or civil service lose their independence by accepting their pensions ? De- cidedly not ; and when they saw several millions voted year after year for the support fo the richer people of the country, he thought the Government might with justice be asked to vote a million or two for the work- ing-classes who had become incapacitated for long con- tinued labour through the infirmities of old age . (Applause.) In conclusion , he proposed success to the Runcorn District of Oddfellows. —Mr. Burrows said that Oddfellowship was established in the Runcorn District in 1828, when the Ebenezer Lodge was formed by the Warrington district. William the Fourth Lodge was opened eight years later. About that time it appeared Warrington, Runcorn, and North- wich constituted one district, but in the early thirties the latter two separated from the former. In 1840 Run- corn settled down for itself, when the Rock of Horeb Lodge was merged in William the Fourth. Their district then comprised two lodges—Ebenezer, with 240 mem- bers, and William the Fourth, 100, with a funeral fund of £228 6;. BJ. In 1852 the Royal Oak Lodge was founded at Widnes, which was officered by Runcorn. Their strength at this period was Ebenezer 201, William IV. 132, and Royal Oak 25, total 358. In 1862 an appli- cation was made to establish the Duke of Devonshire

Lodge, and TW 1-864 the first treasurer was appointed, whilst in 1866 their present occupant of that office, Mr. Robert Wright, was elected. Ir . the same year Bro. Wright proposed the formation of a Past Grand's Lodge, which was carried into effect, and called after the founder,and they were now celebrating its anniversary. The speaker alluded to the opening of two lodges at Appletontafter which he said the present position of the Rtincorn Distrirt was 1,102 adult members, 39 widows,a funeral fund of £489 163. 4d., with an aggregate sick fund of nearly £9,000. He concluded by proposing the health of the district visitors.—Mr. Dunning acknow- ledged this.—Mr. Robert Wright responded to the Past Grands Lodge. He said it had been established 29 years, during which time they had buried 20 members. There were only four of the past grands made on the opening night who were present that evening, namely, Bros. Roberts,Kilcross, Cook, and himself. They stood in the proud position of being worth £5l 83., and the member- ship was 51.—The Chairman submitted "Success to the town and trade of Runcorn."—Mr.Cook,in replying,said there was every reason to believe that the clouds would be dispersed, and success would be experienced by them.—The other toasts were "The Press," and "The Host and Hostess." The speeches were interspersed with songs by Messrs. Harrison, W. E. Dudley,G.H. Walton, Boyers, and Clare. Mr. W. Grounds was the accom- panist.

PRESENTATION TO THE REV. A. J. JAMESON.

There was an interesting gathering in Newtown Schoolroom, on Thursday evening, when the Rev. A. J. Jamescn who has had charge of St. Michaels Church,but who is now vicar of Brinksway, near Stockport, was presented with several tokens in recognition of his three years earnest labours in connection with the church. The presentations took the form of an illuminated address artistically prepared in album form, a silver breakfast dish containing his monogram, and also a revolving book-stand. To Miss Jameson there was pre- sented a silver salver containing a suitable inscription, and a silver tea service. Mr. F. A. Lake occupied the chair and there were also present the Revs. A. J. Jame son,and H. B. Firth, Messrs. J. H. Weston, A. Lightburn Eaton Speakman, J. E. Meredith, James Litter, E Harrison, C. Pownall, E. Ridge, J. Clarke, J. Mort, T. Bowyer, W. Miles, and W. Cropper. The Chairman stated that he had received letters from the Rev. H. C. Moyle and Mr. Mellor apologising for their absence; Continuing, he said they were there to present Mr. Jameson and Miss Jame- son with some tangible expression of their appreciation of their work amongst theta during the past few years. He would like to say how fully he appreciated Mr. Jam- eson's work. He (Mr. Lake had found in him not only a true honest parish clergyman who had been able to put in a word in season and help them in their very best interest, but also a thorough manly friend, and a man who bad taken a modest part in their amusements and recreations. He had made the latter portion of his ministry doubly welcome by bringing his sister amongst t'le 1. He thought her kindly gracious manner had only s xcelled by her strong sense of duty. No self- sacrifice had been too great if she had felt it duty's call. He was sure they all wished both Mr. Jameson and Miss Jameson a successful and happy life. (Applause.) Mr. Weston,in handing the giit to Mr. Jameson,wished to record on behalf of the officers of the church the kindness and the courtesy they had always received from Mr. Jameson for which they desired to give him their heartfelt thanks, and to say how keenly they would feel his departure. (Hear, hear.) The Rev. A. J. Jarrseson thanked the previovs speakers for their kind expressions concerning him, and said he was unable to convey in his poor words how sincerely he thanked all for their kindness to him. His work at Runcorn had extended throughout his ministerial career with the exception of a year and a half at Chester. He began at Weston where he was received most kindly and where he received tokens of the greatest regard, and at St. Michael's after his return from Chester they had ever since treated him in that kind way which had thrown them so closely together. It had been the greatest veasure to work amongst them with such an unanimous people. He then proceeded to speak of the work of the officers of the church, the choir, Sunday school teachers, and in reference to the Vicar, he expressed the hope that they might see him speedily restored to health and actively engaged in parish work again. In the Vicar they had felt that they had a strong man at the head. (Applause.) Mr. M. Lightburn made the presentation to Miss Jameson owing to the indisposition of Mr. Nelson. He said he felt it a privilege to do so. Though no doubt secondary to that of Mr. Weston, yet there was in the presentation to Miss Jameson special considerations. Her work had not been official but purely voluntary. She had no inducements of special gain or of future pre- ferment ; her labour had been for the pure love of the work which her brother had so well led. They desired to express I heir high appreciation of the great interest she had taken in the parish,and he had great pleasure in asking Miss Jameson te accept as a slight token of their esteem the afternoon tea service together with the cordial good wishes of the congregation and friends of St. Michael's with whom she had laboured so harmoniously during the past three years. (Applause.) On behalf of Miss Jameson, the Rev. A. J. Jameson thanked them for the present, alluding t@ his sister's work, and expressing the wish that when any of them came near Stockport they would visit him. The proceedings closed with a vote of thanks to the chairman, on the proposition of Mr. Weston,seconded by Mr. A. Lightburn.

FATAL ACCIDENT TO A RIINCORIAN

On Thursday morning, while steering the Runcorn flat Jacob, of which John Waterworth, of 27, Lightburn-st., Runcorn, is captain, his son Samuel was struck on the head by the tiller and instantly killed. The vessel was at Talycafn, and was laden with stone for the new bridge over the river Conway. Mr. Shaw (missionary) received a request from Captain Waterworth by telegraph to break the sad news to the relatives. This he did, and subse- quently went to Talycafti in deference to the wishes of the parents.—A rumour gained currency the same day that Richard Johnson, of Brook-st., Runcorn, in company with.another man, both of whom are employed by Mr. Richard Abel, carrier, Runcorn, had been drowned in the South-end docks, Liverpool, the previous night. From enquiries made on Thursday it was ascertained that there was RO truth in the rumour as far as Runcorn was concerned.

Tiley, committee, Mr. W. R. Frith, auditor. Votes of thanks to the Chairman were passed, and the meeting then terminated. THE NEW PHOTOGRAPHY.

MIXING EXPERIMENTS AT BERLIN AND PARIS. The "Daily News" Berlin correspondent gives an account of some very interesting and striking experi- ments with the newly discovered Röntgen photographs which took place at Berlin on Tuesday in the" Urania," an institution having for its object the diffusion of know- ledge concerning new inventions and discoveries and all forms of Progress. The correspondent says :—" Dr. Spiess, the experimentalist of the Urania, has from the moment that Professor Rontgen's discovery became known placed his ability at its service, and he has suc- ceeded in producing photographs whieh have turned out better than even those of the discoverer himself, After a brief but very clear lecture about the basis of Professor Rontgen's discovery, and the differences between the x 'rays and the known cathodic rays produced in Crookes's tubes,Dr. Spiess passed on to the experiments. He showed that the'x' rays pass through wood, alu- minium, and other solids, penetrating more easily, how- ever, bodies of light specific gravity than substances of greater density. They pass through wood, for instance, more easily than through glass. Dr. Spiess took a purse put a key and some coins into it, wrapped up the whole in black paper,and laid it on a photographic plate. In order to show the great penetrating power of the rays, he laid on top of the wrapped up purse a board two fingers in thickness. He then exposed the whole to the rays electrically produced in the Routgen tubes. After about fifteen minutes,which were occupied with various very interesting demonstratians,Dr. Spiess took out the plate, developed it,and had it thrown by limelight on to a white curtain. With 'the greatest clearness one now saw the key and the coins,while even the outlines of the purse were scarceiy seen. " Among other photographs shown was a very inter- esting one representing the hand of a servant belonging to the institution. This man years ago ran into his hand a piece of glass, which could not be extracted, and always perpetrated deeper. The photograph of the skeleton of the hand produced by Professor Rontgen's method showed this bit of glass quite distinctly. Another interesting photograph was the following. Dr. Spiess took a box,the sides of which were held together by screws,only the heads of them of course,being visible to the human eye, the other part being inside the wood. In this box he laid a watch chain,and then exposed the whole to the ',2c 'rays. The photograph produced showed the chain, the whole length of all the screws, and not a trace of the wood. Another photograph of a hand showed clearly a broken bone in one of the fingers. Very interesting also was she reproduction of a photograph made by Professor Roatgen of a large metal plate. It appeared that the plate had been broken in different places, then welded together,and so smoothed over that one saw nothing c f the joins. The Röntgen rays brought the fracture to light quite distinctly. "Dr. Spiess warned the audience against premature optimism as regards the Practical employment of Prof. Riintgen's discovery. He nevertheless said that hopes were justified that it would be brought to greater perfection before long. Considering that Professor Röntgen made his first experiments only a few weeks ago, the result achieved was a very favourable one." The matter was also under discussion at the last meeting of the French Academy of Sciences, when M. Poincare exhibited a number of photographs which had been taken of objects placed behind opaque bodies. They were examined with great interest by the assembled savants, and Professor Lannelongue expressed the hope that the method might be found useful in surgery, and announced his intention of endeavouring to make nse of it in his hospital work. M. Poincarci further stated that MM. Oudin and Barthelemy had repeated 'the experi- , mcnts of Professor Röntgen with perfect success.

The usual weekly session of the Earlestown Ebenezer Lodge was held in the Co-operative Hall on Tuesday evening, and was presided over by Bro. W. Hatton, C.T. THEFT OF BEEF AT EARLESTOWN.

At the Town Hall, Newton on Monday morning before Mr. T. J. Gillespie, James Donoghue a collier, of Haydock was charged with ha cing stolen 121bs of beef from the shop of Mrs. Clark, butcher of Market-street, Earlestown. The prisoner was seen by P.C. Bullen to take the beef from the shop win- dow, and the officer at once seized him, but he threw the beef into the street where it was picked up by P.C. Green. Prisoner was remanded for a week on the application of Sergeant Clarke, and was removed to Strangeways gaol.

===== 0002601_18960125_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
SUDDEN DEATH OF A CHILD.

The police on Sunday- received a report of the sudden death of a child named George Wakefield,l4 months old, infant son of Robert Wakefield, of Bank-street, Earlestown. The child had been delicate from birth, but had not been medically at- tended. On Saturday night when it was put to bed, it seemed rather better than usual, but when the parents awoke next morning they found the child dead in bed. The facts were reported to the coroner, but he did not deem an inquest necessary.

ADMISSIONS TO HA.YDOCK COTTAGE HOSPITAL.

Thomas Fairhurst, a collier, aged 39, living at 21, Limekiln-road, llaydock, was on Monday at work in the Pewfall Pit, when he accidently drove a pick into his knee, and had to be removed to the hospital.—James Smith, aged 47, of Mercer-street, Newton, was also admitted into the hospital on Monday, he having had several ribs fractured through being caught by a box in one of the brows of the Wood pit, Haydock.—Thomas Hamilton, a boy aged 9 years, who lived with his parents in Penny-lane, llaydock, was severely burnt on the face and shoulders by the upsetting of a frying pan containing hot fat,and was removed to the hospital on Monday night.

VIADUCT WORKS OFFICE STAFF DINNER.

There was a very pleasant gathering at the Bee Hotel, Liverpool, on Saturday evening, on the occasion of the annual dinner of the Viaduct Works office staff. The party left Earlestown by the 1.34 train, in a saloon specially provided for their accommodation, and on arrival in the city they dis- persed to pass the interval between then and the dinner hour, as they felt disposed. At six o'clock dinner was served at the Bee, and being of an excellent character, it is superfluous to say perhaps, that it was thoroughly enjoyed. The after dinner proceedings were presided over by Mr. T. Ormand, the vice chair being occupied by Mr. C. W. Emmett. Several toasts were proposed and suitably responded to, and songs were given by Messrs. J. Owen, J. Swann, C. W. Vmmett, C. H. Lea and S. Swann. Mr. T. F. Roberts ably filled the onerous post of accompanist. An enjoyable evening was passed and the party reached home about midnight.

SALVATION ARMY, _EARLESTOWN.

A two day's sale of work in connection with the local corps of the Salvation Army, was held in the barracks on Saturday and Monday last. The room had been decorated for the occasion, and the vari- ous stalls were tastefully arranged. There was a variety of articles, most of which had been contri- buted, and it was intended by the sale to raise funds towards the diminution of the slight debt in connection with the corps. There were six stalls, viz.: "Tradesmen's stall, consisting of all kinds of articles kindly given by the tradespeople of Earles- town, which was presided over by Messrs. Williams and Ashcroft; Corps stall,consisting of a variety of useful articles, made principally by the female soldiers and friends, presided over by Mrs. How- gate and Mrs. Evans; bandmen's stall, consisting of articles given expressly for the band expenses by friends, presided ever by Mrs. Harris and Miss Eachus ; junior's stall, consisting of small but pretty and attractive articles, made by the J. S. section of the corps, presided over by Miss C. Glover and Miss E. llolcroft ; refreshment stall, presided over by Mrs. Higson and Mrs. Newman; uniform stall, consisting of various manufactured articles of uniform. The sale was opened by Captain Wilkinson, but the attendance of the public was only meagre.

===== 0002601_18960125_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
GOOD TEMPLARS.

The weekly session of the Hope of Earlestown Lodge of Good Templars was held in the Co-opera- tive Hall on Monday evening. Bro. James Halton, C.T., presided, and there was a full attendance of members, the visitors including Bros. Roberts and Jones,of Widnes. It was arranged to have on open 1 meeting on the Ist ebruary. The lodge was visited by 16 members of the Wesley Hall Lodge,LiverPool, who were introduced with honours. Bro. Rev. J.A. Lee, D.C.T., presided,and other offices were filled by the visitors. Bro. Lee gave a very encouraging address,and short addresses were also given by Bro. West and Sister Gibbons. The fo,iowing pro- gramme was excellently submitted :—Piano solo, "Hosanna," Bro. Lee, jun. ; recitation, "A. woman's story," Sister Bowley ; recitation, " A husband for a shilling," Bro. Rimmer ; song, " When there's love at home," Bro. Macfarlane ; recitation, "The weavers," Miss Bowley ; song, "The rescue,' Bro. J. A. Lee ; song, "A bad boy's diary,' Bro. Lee, jun. ; recitation, "Smoking and joking," Bro. Rimmer. A very pleasant evening was spent,and at the close a vote of thanks was accorded the visitors, on the motion of Bro. C. Drew, seconded by Bro. J. T. Halliday.

MISSIONARY MEETING AT NEWTON. On Saturday evening the annual meeting in aid of the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel in Foreign Parts was held in the Town Hall, Newton. There was a fairly large audience, the Rev. F. W. Johnstone presiding in the absence of the Rev. H. Monk, who has not yet recovered from his accident of a week ago, and he was supported by the Rev.J. Lipscombe„missionary from British Columbia, and his son. Amongst those present were Captain and Mrs. Royse, Mrs. A. Smith, Misses Watkins, Mr.and Mrs. Singleton, and the church officers. The Chair- man prefaced his remarks by reading a letter from Mr. Nickerbon, who is now labouring as a mission- ary in South Africa, and who was formerly a resi- dent in this district. The contents of the mission- ary collecting boxes were Alien made known,, showing a total of £25 ss. 4d., being an increase of £5 13s. 11d. on the previous year. The reading of the names of the holders of the boxes and their amounts elicited much applause, the largest amount £5, coming from the Liverpool Farm School. The Rev. J. Lipscombe then delivered his lecture on the missionary work in British Colom- bia, which was illustrated with several coloured scenes of salmon fiAing, timber cutting, &c. At the conclusion of the address Captain ROySe. R.N., pro- posed a vote of thanks to the rev.gentleman for his interesting lecture, and supported the missionary's statements by relating his own experiences when or in British Columbia. The vote of thanks was suit- ably seconded by Mr. T. May and carried by acclamation. The collection which was afterwards made yielded £2 4s Md.—On Sunday the annual sermons were preached in St. Peter's Church,morn- ing and evening by the lecturer of the previous evening, and an address was given to the scholars in th -! afternoon. The collections during the day mounted to .E 5 17-i. 5d., making a total from all sources of £33 78. 4.1. towards the Society's funds.

OUTDOOR WORK: TREATMENT OF LUNATICS.

Mr. Mclntyre moved the adoption of the Partial:OW tary Committee's recommendations, which were as fol• lows :—" That in the opinion of the Board it is deSir" able that anyone employed under the outdoor Labollf Test Order should not be disfranchised and that & petition be presented to Parliament thereon." Also "That in view of the growing burden imposed upon the rated by the increase of persons confined in public lunatin asylums it is desirable to distinguish between the differ' ent forms of mental disease. For these purposes, there fore, lunatics means persons suffering from acute tranin or other like disease, or labourin4 under delusions chronic lunatics meaning lunatics who, after treatment in an asylum are found to be harmless and not ne( Aarily requiring the strict supervision of an asylum, and also persons suffering from senile decay,softening of thn brain, or other like disease, who are harmless idiots and imbeciles from birth or early age ; that the grant of 49. a week at present given to Boards of Guardians lot pauper lunatic; should also be given for those ma tamed in workhouse wards ' • that the County Council required to appoint visitors for those idiots who are h(1 kept in lunatic asylums, and that it is desirable to e ' press an opinion as to the advisability of extending 4s. grant to idiots."—Mr. Aaron seconded, and it wan carried, Mr. Heywood voting against it.

A NEW WARD.

The Local Government Board authorised the Union tO cause an additional ward for males to be erected at the workhouse of the Union at a cost not exceeding £l5O.

BOOK FOR MARRIED WOMEN The information contained in this book ougltt to be known by every married woman, stud it will not harm the unmarried to read. The book is conveniently Elivided into 12 chapters. The first chapter treats of the changes at puberty,or when a girl becomes a woman. The second chapter treats of marriage from a dodoes standpoint ; points out the best age for marriage, and who should have children and who not, and furnishes useful in- formation that one can ordinarily get only from an in- telligent doctor. The third chapter treats of the marriage of blood relations; and condemns such marriages as a rule. Chapter four treats of the signs of pregnancy. The fifth chapter tells how a woman should live during the pregnant state. The sixth chapter treats of mishaps and how to avoid them. The seventh chapter treats ef maternal impressions mid shows that birthmarks are not due tolongings on the part of the mother, but rather to her poor health. The eighth chapter teaches how to have easy confinements. Certain people believe that women should bring forth in pain and trouble, but the hygienic physician says that confinements can be made comparatively easy if certain rules are obeyed; these rules are given. The ninth chapter treats of the proper management of confinement until the baby is born. The tenth chapter tells how to treat the mother till she is up and about again. The eleventh ohapter treats of sterility ; gives the main causes of it, how these may be overcome and children result The last chapter treats of the "change," a most important article for all women over forty. The book is full of useful information and no book IS written which goes so thoroughly into matters relating to married women. Some may think too much is told ; such can scarcely be the case, for knowledge is power and the means of attaining happiness. The book can be had from Dr. T• R. ALLINSON, 25 Box, 4. t 7., pan ish-place Manchester-square, London, W., in return for a Postal crder for Is. Id.

TAKING COLD. A" Cold "is not necessarily the result of a high or ow temperature. A person may go from a hot bath directly into a cold one, or into snow even, and not take cold. He may remain out in the coldest atmosphere until chilled through, and still not . take cold. On the contrary,he may take cold by pouring a couple of table- spoonfuls of water upon some parts of his dress, or by standing in a door, or before a stove, or by sitting near a window or other opening, where one part of the body is colder than another part. Let it be kept in mind that uniformity of temperature over the whole body is the great thing to be looked after. It is the unequal heat upon ainerent parts of the body that produces Colds, by disturbing the uniform circulation of the blood, which in turn induses congestion of some part. Keep the feet always dry and warm, avoid draughts of air, keep the system braced up by plenty of sleep and good food, and by a powerful Tonic, and you will be proof against a Cold and its results. The very best Medicine, either for reventing or curing a Cold, is O WILYIE EVAN 'S QUININE BITTE RS, DIE VEGETABLE TON IC, Which is the most perfect Vegetable Remedy known,and the most efficacious to strengthen the constitution, en- liven the drooping or deprested spirits,brace the Nerves, and remove all Disorders of the Stomach which are caused by Indigestion, such as Loss of Appetite, distress after Eating, Acidity of Stomach,Eructations, Heaviness and Langour. Disorders of the Liver are often caused by defective Digestion. The potency of the gastric juice in the process of Digestion is wonderfully aided by the powerful ingredients contained in GWILYII EVANS' QUININE BITTERS, THE VEGETABLE TONIC. While the Stomach recovers its normal tone, the putrid undigested food is cleared away, and the poor dyspeptic is relieved of his load of misery, once more enjoys good health, and looks forward to happiness and long life.
